The system must be rebooted for this update to take effect.

Red Hat recommends treating all kernel errata as security-relevant. Given the kernel's fundamental role, any bug has a higher chance of impacting system security, even if that impact only becomes clear after a fix is published. Therefore, Red Hat prioritizes delivering fixes that improve our customers' overall security posture.

Because of this proactive approach, a patch may be associated with a CVE assignment at a future date. Retroactive CVE assignments are always documented in the corresponding errata and on Red Hat's CVE pages. We strongly advise against delaying updates, as doing so may leave your system exposed when protections are already available.

### Microsoft Store App Found Delivering Vinci RAT Capable of Remote System Control
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-27T11:49:34Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: apt_intrusion, malware_botnet
- Summary: Cybersecurity researchers have disclosed details of a cyber attack in which a threat actor leveraged a Microsoft Store application to deliver a remote access trojan (RAT) called Vinci RAT. The intrusion, according to eSentire, begins with a redirection to a bogus site for a sniping tool ("snipp.codes-team[.]com"), from where a Microsoft-signed installer called "Snipping-Tool.exe" is
- Technical details: Attackers abused a Microsoft Store app called Setup Assistant to deploy Vinci RAT, a previously undocumented PowerShell-based remote access trojan.

Cybersecurity researchers have disclosed details of a cyber attack in which a threat actor leveraged a Microsoft Store application to deliver a remote access trojan (RAT) called Vinci RAT .

The intrusion, according to eSentire, begins with a redirection to a bogus site for a sniping tool ("snipp.codes-team[.]com"), from where a Microsoft-signed installer called "Snipping-Tool.exe" is downloaded. Executing the binary leads to the installation of Setup Assistant , an Electron-based application published on the Microsoft Store by ETPS Ltd.

"The application was heavily obfuscated, maintained communication with C2 [command-and-control] infrastructure, monitored the victim's Downloads folder, and supported execution of C2-supplied PowerShell and JavaScript," eSentire said .

"This access was used to launch a malicious PowerShell stager, establish persistence through a scheduled task, and retrieve a previously undocumented PowerShell-based RAT."

Once installed, Setup Assistant acts as a backdoor and is designed to run obfuscated JavaScript that allows it to contact the C2 server at "setup-assistant[.]com" and retrieve arbitrary PowerShell or JavaScript code, ultimately resulting in the deployment of the RAT by means of a stager.

Vinci RAT, as the malware is called, is equipped to fingerprint the host, enumerate installed security tools, facilitate remote and reverse shell access, deliver files, and execute shellcode, DLL, EXE, and PowerShell payloads.

Interestingly, the arbitrary PowerShell or JavaScript execution is triggered by means of two methods -

Monitor for file names in the victim's Downloads directory by matching them against a predefined list specified in a C2-retrieved configuration ("MSTeamsSetup.exe" and "Reader_install.exe"). If a match is found, it sends an HTTP POST request to the C2 server and executes the response.

The C2 server initiates an update action via the URL setup-assistant[.]com/api/update-status?device=<victim_id>"

The Vinci RAT stager is responsible for running a PowerShell command that disables the Windows Antimalware Scan Interface ( AMSI ), generates a unique victim identifier, establishes communication with a C2 server ("monkeysholder[.]info/api.php"), set up persistence via a scheduled task that runs every 10 minutes, and downloads the RAT payload.

Further analysis of the root domain " codes-team[.]com " has identified additional sub-domains that suggest that the threat actors are spoofing software download pages for legitimate applications like Microsoft Teams and Roblox -

As of writing, Setup Assistant is no longer accessible on Microsoft Software. It was first released on June 1, 2026, while the "codes-team[.]com" domain was registered in late March 2026.

It remains unclear whether the threat actors themselves took down the app in response to the public disclosure or whether Microsoft removed it, as eSentire noted that the tech giant had failed to take action despite reporting the app through its abuse form on November 10, 2026, responding that it was "not a violation of our terms."

"The campaign highlights how attackers are combining search-engine redirection, fake software download pages, obfuscated Electron applications, and trusted software distribution channels to gain persistent access to victim environments," the Canadian cybersecurity company said.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Microsoft removes WSUS server role in Windows Server 2028
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-27T11:18:36Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Microsoft will remove the WSUS server role from Windows Server 2028, the next major release of its server operating system. [...]
- Technical details: Microsoft will remove the WSUS server role from Windows Server 2028, the next major release of its server operating system.

Introduced in 2005 as Software Update Services (SUS), WSUS (Windows Server Update Services) lets IT administrators manage and distribute updates for Microsoft products across corporate networks with many Windows devices.

It gives admins centralized control over the updates, rather than having each device download them individually from Microsoft's servers.

Microsoft has officially deprecated WSUS in September 2024 , but said that it still plans to maintain functionality and continue publishing updates through the channel. The change affects enterprise environments that rely on WSUS to manage updates delivered to many devices but does not impact home users or those using Microsoft Configuration Manager.

In a recently updated support document, Microsoft went even further and confirmed that Windows Server 2028 removes the WSUS server role altogether.

"WSUS remains available and supported in earlier Windows Server releases, including Windows Server 2025, for the duration of each release's support lifecycle. You can continue to use WSUS on those releases to manage updates for supported Windows client and Windows Server releases, which includes updates to Windows Server 2028," it said .

"Microsoft recognizes the need to manage updates in disconnected and highly controlled environments. We're invested in supporting these scenarios and we'll share more as the work progresses."

Businesses are now encouraged to adopt cloud-based solutions, such as Windows Autopatch, Microsoft Intune, and Azure Update Manager, for keeping endpoints and servers on their network up to date.

"While the WSUS role remains available in Windows Server 2025, we recommend organizations transition to cloud tools, including including Windows Autopatch and Microsoft Intune for client update management and Azure Update Manager for server update management," Microsoft's Nir Froimovici said in September 2024. "Remember: WSUS remains operational but is no longer investing in new features."

Windows Server 2028 also removes VBScript ( disabled by default in Internet Explorer 11 on Windows 10) and the legacy Windows Management Instrumentation Command-line (WMIC) utility ( deprecated in Windows Server 2012 and Windows 10 21H1) to thwart a wide range of malware and attack tactics abusing them.

Windows Internet Name Service (WINS) support, deprecated with the release of Windows Server 2022 in August 2021, is also being removed in the next Windows Server version, as Microsoft announced a year ago .

Microsoft announced Windows Server 2028 last week at the Ignite 2026 conference, and it can be tested by registering for the Windows Insider Program for Windows Server or applying to enroll in the Windows Server Technology Adoption Program (TAP) for earlier access.

### How to Keep Your Security Picture Current When Attackers Move at AI Speed
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-27T10:32:18Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: Most security metrics are not built to be used. They are built to be shown. Somewhere between the quarterly review and the next status update, a set of numbers gets assembled that looks reassuring in a room and tells almost no one what is actually happening across the environment that week. Someone pulls them together the week before, cleans them up, and presents a picture of a program that is,
- Technical details: Continuous security posture visibility helps teams address outdated metrics, fragmented data, and the growing pace of AI-accelerated threats.

Most security metrics are not built to be used. They are built to be shown. Somewhere between the quarterly review and the next status update, a set of numbers gets assembled that looks reassuring in a room and tells almost no one what is actually happening across the environment that week. Someone pulls them together the week before, cleans them up, and presents a picture of a program that is, as of that one morning, fine.

This is not a failure of effort. Security teams are doing the work, often times more of it than anyone outside the team realizes. The problem is that the reporting cycle rewards the snapshot, and a snapshot is a memory, not a measurement. It tells you how things looked on the day you looked. It says very little about the weeks in between, and risk does not keep to a reporting schedule.

The conversation about security metrics usually jumps straight to which numbers matter. That skips the part that actually hurts. Before anyone can report a single metric, someone has to go and gather it, and today that is a genuinely painful job.

The information needed to describe a security posture is scattered across dozens of systems that were never meant to talk to each other. Cloud accounts, identity providers, endpoints, code repositories, ticketing tools, each one holds a piece. Pulling those pieces together usually means logging into one console after another, exporting spreadsheets, taking screenshots, and stitching it all by hand into something presentable. It takes days. It eats the time of people who were hired to do security, not data entry. And by the time the picture is assembled, parts of it are already out of date, because the environment kept changing while the deck was being built.

So the metric that lands in the review is not really a measurement of where things stand. It is a measurement of where things stood, filtered through however long it took to collect. (And filtered, quietly, toward whatever looked defensible that week.)

Here is why the stale snapshot has gone from a weakness to a real liability. The people trying to get in have changed speed.

AI has handed attackers a serious acceleration. Reconnaissance that used to take a skilled operator days now runs in minutes. Phishing that used to read as obviously fake now arrives fluent and personalized at scale. Known weaknesses get found and probed almost as fast as they appear. The attack side has automated the slow parts, and the result is adversaries who move in hours, iterate constantly, and do not pause politely between your reporting cycles.

Now put the two timelines next to each other. On one side, a picture of your security that is gathered by hand, a few times a year, and is slightly stale the moment it is finished. On the other, an adversary moving at machine speed, every day, including the long quiet stretch between your last review and your next one. That gap was always uncomfortable. Against AI-accelerated attackers, it is the whole game.

The fix is not a better slide. It is being able to see where you actually stand, continuously, without a person having to go assemble it first.

That is worth saying plainly, because it is easy to confuse with detection. This is not about catching the attacker in the act. It is about never losing sight of your own environment in the first place, knowing what you have, what is covered, what changed, and where the gaps are, as a living picture rather than a quarterly photograph. Continuous visibility into the real environment, pulled straight from the systems that already hold the data, is what turns a security posture from something you reconstruct under deadline into something you can simply look at, any day, and trust.

This is the problem Anecdotes was built to solve. Instead of a person logging into one console after another and rebuilding the picture by hand, it connects directly to the systems a company already runs, the cloud accounts, identity providers, code repositories, and the hundreds of other tools where the real state actually lives, and pulls the information straight from the source. The picture assembles itself and stays current, so the question stops being how many days it will take to gather and becomes simply what the data says right now. And to be clear about what that is and is not, it does not chase the attacker for you. It makes sure you are never the last to know what your own environment looks like.

When the picture is always current, the work changes shape. Nobody spends the week before the review gathering. The number is just there, today's number, and the team spends its time acting on what it says instead of proving it exists.

None of this requires rebuilding everything at once, and anyone who says it does is selling something. Pick one thing worth seeing clearly, coverage, say, or how long exposures stay open. Connect it to the source so it keeps itself current. Watch it for a month, and let it tell you something uncomfortable.

Security is not a thing that is true on review day. It is true, or it is not, on an ordinary Tuesday, when no one is looking and last quarter's slide is gathering dust in a shared drive. And on that Tuesday, the attackers are not waiting. The teams that stay ahead are not the ones with the best-looking dashboards. They are the ones who can see where they stand at any moment, without having to go dig for it first.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Drupal warns of new site takeover flaw exploited in attacks
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-27T10:10:15Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-132165, CVE-2026-136716 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: The Drupal security team says attackers are exploiting a second critical vulnerability that lets them breach and hijack unpatched websites. [...]
- Technical details: The Drupal security team says attackers are exploiting a second critical vulnerability that lets them breach and hijack unpatched websites.

Last week, Drupal patched a remote code execution (RCE) flaw (tracked as CVE-2026-132165 / Fail2Install ) that lets anonymous users take over vulnerable sites by abusing the Drupal installer.

While the security issue wasn't initially flagged as being abused in the wild, an update to the original security advisory warned that threat actors began exploiting it one day after patches were released.

The new security issue ( CVE-2026-136716 ), patched one week later, addresses attack vectors that were left unpatched and also lets attackers hijack vulnerable Drupal sites.

"However, certain sites in unusual or incomplete install states remain vulnerable to site takeover following that advisory, including production sites. It is not apparent in the user interface if a site is in the affected vulnerable state, and sites that are already compromised may no longer be in the vulnerable state," Drupal said on Wednesday.

"This is being exploited in the wild. Any site that left its installer remotely accessible following SA-CORE-2026-017 may still have been compromised."

While it has yet to share further details regarding these attacks, Drupal warned users that any unsecured website may have been compromised and advised admins to ensure the user 1 email address is one they control, and to consider resetting it.

They should also check site logs for unauthorized access, focusing on unexpected changes to or by the user 1 account, and follow this guide if they find evidence the site may have been compromised.

Those who can't immediately upgrade to Drupal 10.6.23 , 11.3.23 , or 11.4.14 should secure the installer on all new and existing sites by blocking access to /core/install.php in the web server configuration or the .htaccess file in the web root.

The US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) has flagged six Drupal security flaws as being abused in the wild since November 2021, two of which have also been exploited in ransomware attacks.

### SAP confirms security incident after suspicious GitHub activity
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-27T09:56:36Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: SAP has confirmed to BleepingComputer that it took urgent action to protect its customers after detecting suspicious activity in its IT environment. [...]
- Technical details: SAP has confirmed to BleepingComputer that it took urgent action to protect its customers after detecting suspicious activity in its IT environment.

The German software giant said its evidence so far does not show downstream compromise of customer systems or data.

Based in Walldorf, Germany, SAP is one of the world’s largest enterprise software vendors, with 110,000 employees in 157 countries, 300 million subscribers, and over $40 billion in annual revenue .

Der Spiegel reported earlier today, based on an internal SAP memo it saw, that the software firm was hit by a cyberattack it tracks under the code name “Project Granite.”

The software company reportedly asked all employees to keep the project details confidential.

According to the German outlet, sources within the company said the attackers breached SAP’s GitHub environment, possibly with help from AI tools.

Insider sources say that the attacker managed to steal authentication tokens from SAP employees and leveraged them to copy and also modify SAP source code on GitHub.

BleepingComputer has contacted SAP for a statement on the reported breach, and the company acknowledged the incident and said it had taken steps to protect its customers.

“SAP has identified suspicious activity and promptly implemented the necessary measures to protect its customers and preserve the security of its services,” a company representative told BleepingComputer.

“Based on our investigation to date, we have found no evidence of unauthorized access to customer business data.”

“Our customers trust us to protect their data, and that remains our highest priority. In a highly dynamic threat environment, we will continue to strengthen our security to provide the secure and resilient environment our customers depend on.”

SAP did not specify the environments where the suspicious activity was observed, or whether customers downloading code from its GitHub repositories or performing updates that are directly fetched or indirectly fed by those repositories should take any action.

BleepingComputer has asked SAP about this, but a spokesperson declined to share any further information.

Meanwhile, Germany’s Federal Office for Information Security (BSI) issued a public warning yesterday about agentic AI tools that empower cyberattacks, such as ARTEX, CAIRN, and PentAGI.

BSI cautioned organizations in the country to focus on responding to security incidents and ensure they follow all recommended security-strengthening practices.

This warning comes shortly after the national cybersecurity agencies in Switzerland and Italy reported the first detections of large-scale AI-driven cyberattacks targeting organizations in the critical infrastructure sector.

### Hackers Trojanize Official Q-Dir and DesktopOK Downloads to Deliver Remote Access Malware
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-27T09:29:30Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: supply_chain, malware_botnet
- Summary: Cybersecurity researchers have disclosed details of a software supply chain attack that has tampered with Q-Dir and DesktopOK installation files distributed via the official SoftwareOK website. "Evidence indicates that malicious files were generated not only when users downloaded the programs directly from the official download page but also during the update process of previously installed
- Technical details: Trojanized Q-Dir and DesktopOK installers from SoftwareOK's official website deploy a Python RAT capable of remote command execution.

Cybersecurity researchers have disclosed details of a software supply chain attack that has tampered with Q-Dir and DesktopOK installation files distributed via the official SoftwareOK website.

"Evidence indicates that malicious files were generated not only when users downloaded the programs directly from the official download page but also during the update process of previously installed legitimate programs," South Korean cybersecurity company AhnLab said .

Q-Dir, also called the Quad Directory Explorer, is a free, lightweight file manager for Windows developed by SoftwareOK. According to its website, DesktopOK is a free Windows utility designed to save and restore desktop icon positions.

The unauthorized modifications are designed such that the malicious activity is challenging to detect when running the programs, while simultaneously retrieving and deploying a Python-based remote control malware (RAT) from an external server.

Once installed, the RAT payload is capable of executing shell commands and Python code, transferring files, capturing screenshots, and collecting system metadata based on commands issued by the attacker.

The following product versions have been confirmed to be poisoned, given the discrepancies in the SHA-256 hashes of the installer files and the presence of additional code that triggers additional file downloads and automatic execution -

The exact mechanism that allowed the installation files to be replaced with a trojanized version remains unknown.

"The malicious installer runs the legitimate Q-Dir or DesktopOK to lower the user's suspicion, while simultaneously downloading and installing a Python RAT package from an external server," AhnLab said.

Specifically, this involves downloading two payloads from "mail.c1a2[.]top" -

dsv2_py39_agent.7z, a package containing the RAT and Python runtime environment

The attack chain is fairly straightforward in that it uses the 7-Zip tool binary to extract and run the Python RAT. The malware then sets up persistence on the host by means of a scheduled task that impersonates Microsoft Edge to fly under the radar. The task is configured to execute the RAT by launching a Python script named "py_client_dsv2.py."

Upon execution, the RAT establishes communication with its command-and-control (C2) server ("8.210.69[.]51:9876") to receive tasks and run them. It supports the following features -

It also gathers a list of running processes and compares it against an internal list of various security products used in South Korea and abroad, including AhnLab, ESTSecurity, Hauri, CrowdStrike, SentinelOne, Trend Micro, Avast, McAfee, ESET, Symantec, Kaspersky, Bitdefender, Sophos, Fortinet, Elastic, and Malwarebytes.

Information about identified security programs is transmitted to the C2 server, so as to help the attackers better plan the next stages of the attack or serve payloads that can bypass detection.

The activity has not been attributed to any known threat actor. However, AhnLab said it identified Chinese language comments in the source code, suggesting that the malware authors or attackers may have used a Chinese-speaking environment.

"Users who are currently using Q-Dir or DesktopOK, or who have recently installed or updated these programs, should verify the hash values of the downloaded files and check their systems for any traces of RAT installation or execution," AhnLab said.

"However, simply uninstalling and reinstalling the program may not remove a Python RAT that has already been installed. Therefore, you should also check for suspicious Python execution environments, scheduled tasks, related files, and traces of external network communication, update your antivirus engine to the latest version, and then perform a full system scan."

### Microsoft pushes emergency hotpatch to fix Windows Server crashes
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-27T09:08:16Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Microsoft has released an emergency hotpatch update to address Windows Server 2025 crashes triggered after installing this month's security hotpatch update (KB5131077). [...]
- Technical details: Microsoft has released an emergency hotpatch update to address Windows Server 2025 crashes triggered after installing this month's security hotpatch update (KB5131077).

Microsoft has released an emergency hotpatch update to address Windows Server 2025 crashes triggered after installing this month's security hotpatch update ( KB5131077 ).

According to Microsoft, the KB5138318 out-of-band update should address stop errors with 0x139 codes triggered on affected servers when IPv6 routes expire.

"Microsoft has released a new Windows out-of-band (OOB) update today, November 25, 2026, to address an issue affecting a limited number of Windows Servers with Router Advertisement (RA) enabled that receive time-bound IPv6 routes," it said in a message center update.

"Affected devices might stop unexpectedly with stop error 0x139 (KERNEL_SECURITY_CHECK_FAILURE) when a route timer expires. This issue affects some Windows Server 2025 devices after installing the November 2026 Windows security hotpatch update."

This emergency non-security update is now rolling out to affected Windows Server 2025 devices enrolled in hotpatch updates through Windows Update.

KB5138318 will download and install automatically and will take effect without requiring IT administrators or users to restart affected devices.

The November 2026 hotpatch updates are also causing issues for Windows 11 users, who are experiencing installation and launch issues affecting Office and third-party applications, as well as printing issues on systems running Forcepoint's DLP Endpoint Agent data loss prevention solution after installing this month's hotpatch on Windows 11 systems.

Windows Autopatch reached public preview in early June after an initial unveil in April 2022 and has been generally available since July 2022 .

Microsoft announced earlier this year, in March, that it would turn on hotpatch security updates by default for all eligible Windows devices managed through the Microsoft Graph API and Microsoft Intune, after the May 2026 Windows security updates are installed.

One month later, Microsoft fixed a Windows Autopatch bug that was causing driver updates restricted by administrative policies to deploy on some Autopatch-managed Windows devices in the European Union.

### New NFS2ROOT Linux flaw can let attackers gain root privileges
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-27T08:06:58Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-99362 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: ​A 12-year-old critical vulnerability in the Linux kernel's Network File System (NFS) server, tracked as CVE-2026-99362, allows remote attackers to gain root privileges. [...]
- Technical details: ​A 12-year-old critical vulnerability in the Linux kernel's Network File System (NFS) server, tracked as CVE-2026-99362, allows remote attackers to gain root privileges.

Found by ANSSI's Jérémy Jean (who submitted a fix in August ), the flaw has been dubbed NFS2ROOT by cybersecurity firm OpenSec, which shared a full remote-root exploit and a patch with the NFSD maintainers in late September.

The vulnerability has existed since Linux kernel version 3.16 ( released in March 2014 ), and it stems from a heap buffer overflow weakness in the Linux NFS server (nfsd).

NFS2ROOT affects systems with a Linux kernel older than 6.12.117, 6.18.61, 7.3.2, or 7.4-rc1 if nfsd (v4.1 or 4.2) is loaded and running and TCP port 2049 is reachable over the Internet.

Successful exploitation lets remote threat actors without privileges corrupt kernel memory and gain root access to vulnerable Linux servers exposed online through low-complexity attacks that don't require user interaction.

In NFS2ROOT attacks, the threat actors can exploit this flaw by sending maliciously crafted requests that cause the NFS server to write data beyond an allocated memory buffer. This lets them read kernel memory, bypass KASLR (which protects kernel memory addresses), and place a callback that can execute a command in a released memory area.

When the attacker closes the NFS connection, the kernel executes the embedded command with root privileges, allowing the attacker to gain root control of the unpatched Linux server.

​Although there are no reports of CVE-2026-99362 exploitation in the wild, security teams should apply the latest kernel security updates from their Linux distribution vendor immediately to block potential attacks, then reboot the system to ensure the patched kernel is in use.

Those who can't apply a patch immediately should restrict access to the NFS server over TCP port 2049 to trusted clients or networks. Additionally, if NFS sharing is not required on the system, admins can stop and disable the nfs-server service in /etc/nfs.conf.

"Port 2049 sits open on file servers, NAS appliances, build farms and Kubernetes storage nodes. Since 2014, the Linux kernel listening on it has let anyone who can reach it write into kernel memory before logging in," OpenSec said . "Kernel hardening does not remove the overflow. Patch every host."

While the NFS2ROOT attacks have no network signatures, security teams can still monitor for potential signs of exploitation attempts by checking for kernel warnings from nfsd4_check_resp_size, shells spawned by kernel workers on a file server, bursts of short NFSv4.1 sessions from one source, and kernel oopses with nfsd4_*, svc_* or call_usermodehelper* frames, according to OpenSec.

NFS2ROOT is the latest in a long series of Linux privilege escalation security flaws disclosed and patched since the start of the year, including RefluXFS , CIFSwitch , Dirty Frag , Fragnesia , PinTheft , Copy Fail , Pack2TheRoot , and DirtyDecrypt/DirtyCBC .

### Switzerland Confirms AI-Assisted Cyberattacks Breached Critical Infrastructure Operators
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-27T06:04:20Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: Attackers using AI agents have broken into computer systems at Swiss organizations in four of five confirmed attacks, the first such cases in the country, the country's cybersecurity agency said yesterday. Three of the attacks targeted critical infrastructure operators, and two of those succeeded, with no major disruptions reported. The victims have not been named, and it is not known who is
- Technical details: Switzerland confirms its first five cyberattacks using AI agents, four of which succeeded, including two against critical infrastructure.

Attackers using AI agents have broken into computer systems at Swiss organizations in four of five confirmed attacks, the first such cases in the country, the country's cybersecurity agency said yesterday.

Three of the attacks targeted critical infrastructure operators, and two of those succeeded, with no major disruptions reported. The victims have not been named, and it is not known who is behind the attacks.

Florian Schütz , who directs Switzerland's National Cyber Security Centre, gave the figures in an interview with public broadcaster SRF.

All five cases were reported in recent days. Small and medium-sized companies were among the organizations hit, and they came forward voluntarily.

The agency's first notice, a security advisory published on Tuesday, said the agents had compromised the victims' IT systems in every case known at that point. Neither the notice nor yesterday's broadcast interview explains why the count changed.

"These cases represent a shift in the cyber threat landscape," the advisory said.

The targeted operators are in at least two sectors. Electricity, drinking water, and telecommunications suppliers are examples of what Switzerland counts as critical infrastructure, according to a news agency report on the interview.

The attacks involve what the Swiss agency calls agentic offensive frameworks, AI agents that find security weaknesses and carry out attacks on their own. Its advisory named ARTEX and CyberStrikeAI as examples of agents reachable over the internet, not as the tools used in the five cases.

The agents are often used to scan for known, unpatched weaknesses and can run many attacks in parallel, the agency says. When defenses respond, the agents switch methods, leaving companies significantly less time to detect an intrusion and respond.

The number of AI attack tools freely reachable online has nearly doubled since October, according to the agency. The figure counts available tools, not attacks or victims.

Raphael Reischuk , co-founder of Switzerland's National Test Institute for Cybersecurity, expects a larger wave of attacks and says companies underestimate the risk.

Italy's national computer security incident response team, CSIRT Italia, described mass compromise campaigns in an official bulletin this week. In the cases it observed firsthand, AI-directed autonomous agents broke into a large number of systems within a few hours.

The agents adapt their actions within seconds, run in parallel without human input, and chain vulnerabilities, misconfigurations, and compromised credentials together, the bulletin says. It names ARTEX , CyberStrikeAI , PentAGI , and DeepSeek Harness , an open-source agent whose Red Team Mode uses a built-in prompt to get past the underlying model's safety limits.

Each action on its own looks like normal user or API traffic. Detection depends on context: where requests come from, how often they arrive, how much data moves, and the order in which things happen.

Britain's National Cyber Security Centre reported the same pattern in agency guidance this week. "Since September 2026, the NCSC has seen a marked rise in AI-assisted cyber attacks," it said.

Some incidents caused downtime on internet-facing services, and others gave attackers a foothold for further agent-driven activity. The weaknesses are old ones: credentials committed to code repositories, misconfigured infrastructure-as-code, default or missing authentication, and weaknesses in APIs.

What has changed is the speed, persistence, and low cost of the attacks, the British agency says. The frameworks are also noisy, which makes them easier to detect.

The European Commission has already been hit. Attackers using ARTEX broke into its human resources, medical reimbursement, and translation systems, the Commission has confirmed, in an earlier intrusion first detected on October 22.

### Unpatched Cacti Zero-Day Enables RCE in Just Four HTTP Requests via Guest Access
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-27T05:48:20Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: active_exploitation
- Summary: Details have emerged about a new zero-day vulnerability in Cacti, an open-source network monitoring tool, that could be exploited to achieve remote code execution. The proof-of-concept (PoC) exploit, which has yet to be assigned a CVE identifier, has been tracked as 4HTTP2SHELL and 4HTTP2ROOT. "I found a path from a fresh, unauthenticated HTTP session to command execution as the Cacti server
- Technical details: A Cacti 1.2.31 zero-day PoC demonstrates unauthenticated remote code execution in four HTTP requests when guest access is enabled.

Details have emerged about a new zero-day vulnerability in Cacti , an open-source network monitoring tool, that could be exploited to achieve remote code execution.

The proof-of-concept (PoC) exploit, which has yet to be assigned a CVE identifier, has been tracked as 4HTTP2SHELL and 4HTTP2ROOT .

"I found a path from a fresh, unauthenticated HTTP session to command execution as the Cacti server user in four requests," a security researcher who goes by the online alias "ek0ms savi0r" said . "No login. No valid API key. No phishing. Just four HTTP requests against the default guest surface."

The shortcoming affects Cacti Core 1.2.31, the latest version , when the guest account is enabled. It's worth noting that the guest account is disabled by default starting with Cacti version 0.8.7.

At a high level, the vulnerability allows an attacker to abuse the guest access to write an unvalidated value (i.e., non-numeric text) into a setting called "preview_graphs_per_page" via "auth_profile.php?action=update_data." The configuration parameter controls how many graphs are displayed per page when viewing graphs in Preview Mode.

The crafted value takes the form of arbitrary SQL strings that can be turned into a second-order SQL injection by taking advantage of the fact that Cacti does not validate that the user-provided input is actually an integer value. This, in turn, opens the door for arbitrary SQL query execution when the graph preview page ("graph_view.php") is loaded with default filters.

The SQL query demonstrated by "ek0ms savi0r" is an UPDATE statement to modify a record in Cacti's poller table . In the final stage of the attack, the realtime poller endpoint ("graph_realtime.php") is invoked from the same guest session, which causes the modified row from the poller table to be read and passed as input to PHP's popen() function without any allowlist or sanitization checks.

The researcher has pointed out four failures that ultimately make it possible to achieve unauthenticated remote code execution -

The guest can write a numeric setting as an arbitrary string

Cacti's PHP PDO database driver accepts stacked statements through the SQL LIMIT clause

Successful exploitation of the flaw can allow an attacker to compromise the Cacti server and provide a pivot point to the target network as it exposes sensitive data, or even suppress alerts by modifying the poller table.

"Because Cacti often stores credentials and connection details for routers, switches, servers, and other devices across a network, an attacker who reaches this level of command execution can gain a foothold reaching far beyond the monitoring server itself," watchTowr said in an advisory.

In the absence of a fix, users are advised to disable the guest account and restrict access to the Cacti web interface to mitigate potential risks. It's also essential to review poller configuration entries for unexpected command values and apply the fixes as soon as they become available.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### OpenAI warns GPT-6.2 Astra will hit usage limits 40% faster because it fixed a bug
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-26T21:06:22Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: OpenAI confirmed that it fixed a 'bug' that incorrectly discounted GPT-6.2 usage, which means you'll now hit GPT usage limits 40% faster than before. [...]
- Technical details: OpenAI confirmed that it fixed a 'bug' that incorrectly discounted GPT-6.2 usage, which means you'll now hit GPT usage limits 40% faster than before.

In a post on X, Tibo Sottiaux, who works on Codex and ChatGPT at OpenAI, admitted to a billing mistake that incorrectly discounted GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol usage for most users.

OpenAI says it has fixed the issue but warned that the same tasks will now consume more of users' allowances.

"We've fixed this now, so the same work will use more of your plan's usage allowance," Sottiaux explained.

"Comparing to last week, you will reach your limits 40% faster on GPT-6.2 Astra and 25% faster on Sol."

However, OpenAI says both models will still offer better usage efficiency than their predecessors, GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol.

OpenAI says it won't charge users for the usage difference caused by the mistake, but it is offering something in return for the disruption.

"As a small token to apologize for the mistake, we'll be adding one banked reset for everyone on Go, Plus, Pro, and Business," Sottiaux said.

I can confirm that a new reset has been added to ChatGPT, and it expires a month later. So, if you go to ChatGPT Settings > Usage and click Reset, you can restore your exhausted usage limits.

As much as I appreciate OpenAI giving its users a free usage reset, it's important to understand that this isn't equivalent to getting 40% less usage.

You're still going to use your tokens much faster, and it's unclear when OpenAI will reduce the costs.

It is worth noting that the change affects GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol usage, while other models retain their existing usage calculations.

### ThreatsDay: 10M NGINX Services at Risk, 250+ Malicious Extensions, AI Server Hijacking + 17 More Stories
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-26T19:39:57Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: A website can look perfectly fine to the person running it. Log out, visit the same page, and things might look different. Something similar is happening with browser extensions, where even the way you find and install one can change what it does. Checking whether software works as expected only tells you so much. Then there’s the question of who gets to use what. Stolen logins are opening
- Technical details: This week's ThreatsDay tracks hidden browser threats, critical NGINX flaws, credential theft, ransomware trends, and major cybercrime busts.

A website can look perfectly fine to the person running it. Log out, visit the same page, and things might look different. Something similar is happening with browser extensions, where even the way you find and install one can change what it does. Checking whether software works as expected only tells you so much.

Then there’s the question of who gets to use what. Stolen logins are opening doors across connected systems, while some AI servers are sitting online for anyone to use at someone else’s expense. Even software responsible for keeping the power on has found its way into an attack.

Meanwhile, authorities are taking down large scam operations, extortion gangs are making comebacks, and ransomware activity is picking up. Some of the more worrying findings this week involve things most people would have little reason to suspect. The details are worth a look.

The threats change every week. Subscribe, and we’ll alert you when each new ThreatsDay Bulletin is out.

Todoist has disclosed that an attacker gained unauthorized access to its production systems and retrieved some customer information on November 23, 2026. The incident affects 6% of its users. "Between November 22 and 23, an attacker used a stolen credential associated with our version control system to modify automated workflows in three repositories and obtain additional credentials," it said . During this time, they could also read our source code. Our investigation found no changes to application code. One of the credentials they obtained allowed them to change how employees access our internal systems. The attacker used it to take over an employee’s work account and gain access to our cloud infrastructure. On November 23, the attacker downloaded a file containing account information for some early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method. They also made targeted queries for account tokens, searched some customer content for credentials, and accessed email information through connected Todoist for Outlook accounts."

CrowdStrike said it will acquire Zscaler in a deal worth $43.9 billion, making it the biggest cybersecurity acquisition on record. "Security used to be about keeping the adversary out. Today, safety is required to let AI in," CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said . "The network used to connect people to places. Now it connects agents to data. With Zscaler, we're building cybersecurity's operating system: a single control plane to deploy AI, control AI, and enable any agentic application to run safely. We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen."

Microsoft's Edge Extensions Security Team has documented SleeperWidget, a set of over 250 extensions across Microsoft Edge Add-ons, Chrome Web Store, and Mozilla Add-ons that work as advertised unless installed through a paid search ad. Once installed that way, they collect browsing data, inject ads, and change shopping links. "Its extensions pose as maps, recipe finders, package trackers, and other everyday tools. Most deliver the features they advertise, which makes hidden behavior easier to miss," it said . "In the extensions we analyzed, installation through a paid-advertising funnel also activated browsing-data collection, ad injection, and shopping-link modification."

An analysis of 20,354 internet-facing hosts has revealed that thousands of servers where AI services or notebook APIs have been deployed and left accessible without authentication, potentially enabling attackers to abuse them for their own purposes. "More than half of the exposed Ollama servers in this scan have already been touched by someone who is not the owner," Rapid7 said . "79% of exposed Ollama hosts list at least one :cloud model, mostly GLM, DeepSeek, MiniMax and Kimi variants. An exposed, signed-in server can provide access to someone else's paid subscription, with the bill landing on the owner. This is the self-hosted version of LLMjacking, and it requires no exploit at all."

SonicWall Capture Labs has disclosed details of a Java-based loader from SilentNet , a Java-based remote access trojan and infostealer sold as a malware-as-a-service offering that's advertised on YouTube. The JAR masquerades as a mod for Minecraft Fabric and targets players who download mods from external sources. "Once loaded by the game, it pulls the player's Minecraft session token from memory. It then gets its command-and-control (C2) address from a smart contract on the Polygon blockchain, and downloads a portable Python runtime hidden inside a PNG image to run a second-stage infostealer," SoniWall said . "Every string in the JAR is hidden by per-character arithmetic; it resolves hostnames through DNS-over-HTTPS, and it talks to its C2 through a custom HTTPS client that skips certificate validation."

Threat actors are using DeepSite, Hugging Face's AI website builder, to create dozens of phishing and credential-stealing pages on Hugging Face Spaces impersonating 28 brands, including Microsoft, Google, PayPal, Chase, and Coinbase. "29 of the pages were built with DeepSite, Hugging Face’s AI website builder," Aikido said . "One Microsoft login clone took about 90 minutes of plain-language prompts, and other operators asked the builder to hide their pages from crawlers or send stolen logins to Telegram."

According to VulnCheck, requests to steal credentials made up 13% of all vulnerability exploitation recorded between January and November 2026. Credential-harvesting attempts appeared across 127 of 694 CVEs observed in its honeypot network, with open-source software and AI developer tools like Apache, Laravel, Langflow, Flowise, MLflow, and n8n taking up 61% of the CVEs associated with credential-harvesting attempts. "Attackers search application files, logs and environment variables for database passwords, cloud credentials and API keys, with file-read and SSRF vulnerabilities providing opportunities without code execution," VulnCheck said , adding it "observed 1,756 attempts targeting AI provider and coding agent credentials from 406 IP addresses across 27 CVEs."

A previously unknown ransomware and data-extortion group calling itself BYOD has claimed at least nine organizations between October 5 and November 9, 2026. "Its attacks have had real-world consequences, including ambulance diversion, more than 30 clinics closed, and campus-wide device shutdowns," TrendAI said . "BYOD runs a pressure campaign alongside the intrusion: audio messages on infected computers, public leak-site posts, statements framing its attacks as holding organizations accountable, and outreach to individual victims." Although the complete intrusion chain remains to be analyzed, evidence points to credential compromise and a remote access trojan on an employee's computer for initial access.

A malware campaign has been observed installing a fake WordPress plugin built almost entirely by a name generator, including its title, directories, files, and functions (e.g., trijavascriptly, ultravueal, maxdataic, and quadmicroservicesion). "Behind the generated names is a second-stage delivery platform that runs arbitrary JavaScript in the browser of every anonymous visitor to an infected site, routes that traffic through the victim's own domain, and lies dormant for logged-in users," Wordfence said . The malware has been discovered twice, once in January 2026 and another in September 2026. Exactly how the plugin is installed is not known, but investigation shows that it's likely via a compromise of an administrator account.

The U.S. Department of Justice (DoJ) has announced that over 17.3 million scam accounts have been deplatformed on Meta, 700 fraudulent crypto investment websites seized, and 12 U.S. arrest warrants issued as part of efforts to tackle foreign and domestic scams over the past six months. The Strike Force has traced and restrained $1,082,260,156.82 in cryptocurrency stolen from U.S. victims. "Our Scam Center Strike Force has continued its work investigating cyber-enabled fraud perpetrated against Americans by transnational organized crime groups, emanating out of Asia and attacking America's main street," said U.S. Attorney Jeanine Ferris Pirro. "The Strike Force teams focus on identifying and pursuing key leaders — including Chinese organized crime affiliates operating in Cambodia, Laos, and Burma, and rapidly expanding around the globe — to bring them to justice." FBI Director Kash Patel said the Operation Blackout scam demolition initiative has "seized nearly $17 billion in illicit funds, taken more than 14,000 digital devices, directly warned over 11,000 Americans from scam attempts, and prevented more than $690 million in potential victim losses." Over 29 scam compounds operating across Asia, Africa, and the Middle East have been dismantled and m

- Summary: NordVPN's 2026 Black Friday and Cyber Monday deal is now live, offering savings of up to 78% on VPN subscriptions, plus three extra months of protection. [...]
- Technical details: NordVPN's 2026 Black Friday and Cyber Monday deal is now live, offering savings of up to 78% on VPN subscriptions, plus three extra months of protection.

The NordVPN Black Friday deal is an great opportunity for anyone looking to enhance their online security and privacy while saving money on a long-term VPN subscription.

Whether you're looking to protect your personal information when connecting to public Wi-Fi, access your favorite streaming services while traveling abroad, or prevent third parties from monitoring your internet traffic, NordVPN offers a comprehensive set of privacy and security features.

The VPN provider also offers additional cybersecurity tools, including file download scanning for malware, phishing protection, a password manager, and encrypted cloud storage, depending on your subscription plan.

With a 30-day money-back guarantee, you can also try NordVPN and see whether it meets your needs.

This year's NordVPN Black Friday promotion offers savings of up to 78%, plus three extra months when purchasing a two-year subscription, giving you 27 months of VPN protection.

The advertised 78% savings are based on comparing the introductory monthly price of NordVPN's highest-tier two-year plan, including the additional months, with the current monthly price of its annual renewal.

Actual savings vary depending on the subscription plan and introductory term you select.

The promotion is particularly appealing to those looking for more than just a VPN, with NordVPN's higher-tier subscriptions bundling additional cybersecurity and privacy tools into a single subscription.

One of NordVPN's biggest selling points is its extensive global server network, which offers subscribers thousands of servers to choose from when connecting to the VPN.

High-speed connections using its WireGuard-based NordLynx protocol.

This extensive network gives users plenty of options when choosing a VPN server, whether they're looking for a nearby server for everyday browsing or connecting to a location back home while traveling abroad.

NordVPN's NordLynx protocol is designed to minimize the performance impact of using a VPN, making it suitable for streaming, gaming, and other bandwidth-intensive activities.

A single NordVPN account also supports up to 10 devices, allowing you to protect multiple computers, smartphones, and tablets without purchasing separate subscriptions.

You can also install NordVPN on a compatible router to protect your home network.

Depending on the subscription plan you choose, NordVPN's features and additional services include:

VPN encryption: Encrypts your internet connection to help prevent third parties from monitoring your online activity.

### Fake paid expert interviews impersonate brands to steal Google logins
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-26T17:15:27Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: apt_intrusion, identity, phishing
- Summary: An ongoing phishing campaign dubbed "GoodGuys" impersonates major brands, including Google, Nvidia, Accenture, and SpaceX, to steal corporate Google and Okta credentials using fake invitations to paid expert interviews and webinars. [...]
- Technical details: An ongoing phishing campaign dubbed "GoodGuys" impersonates major brands, including Google, Nvidia, Accenture, and SpaceX, to steal corporate Google and Okta credentials using fake invitations to paid expert interviews and webinars.

The attacks offer recipients up to $600 for a 45-minute interview in an attempt to get them to sign in with their work accounts to participate.

The campaign was discovered by cybersecurity firm Push Security , which has tracked it since October 13 and found that they impersonate eight companies: Accenture, American Express, Coca-Cola, Culver's, Google Cloud, Nvidia, SpaceX, and Spotify.

The threat actors abuse legitimate email and event platforms, including HireVue, SendGrid, Mandrill, and Cvent, to distribute phishing links.

In one attack, a phishing email sent through HireVue invited recipients to attend a Google Cloud webinar about deploying AI applications into production.

The threat actors use domains themed around interviews, webinars, and research studies, creating brand-specific subdomains to host the phishing pages, such as:

Clicking the phishing links typically takes recipients to a CAPTCHA challenge before displaying a website impersonating the company that supposedly sent the invitation.

Each website features customized branding and lures, with the Nvidia phishing site offering $500 to participate in an AI feedback study and the Accenture site offering $600 for an expert interview.

The sites ultimately prompt visitors to sign in with their Google accounts to validate their invitations, allowing the attackers to steal their credentials. The phishing kit specifically targets corporate accounts and rejects email addresses associated with Gmail, Yahoo, and Outlook.

Unlike adversary-in-the-middle (AiTM) phishing kits that use reverse proxies to relay legitimate login pages, GoodGuys uses fake authentication forms controlled by an attacker-controlled server.

After victims enter their Google credentials, the phishing page can display various multifactor authentication (MFA) challenges, including requests for SMS codes, authenticator codes, and Google sign-in prompts requiring users to select a matching number on their phones.

The kit also includes fake Okta authentication forms and support for Okta Verify push notifications.

Push Security believes the threat actors manually attempt to log in using the stolen credentials, instructing the phishing page to display whatever MFA or security challenges Google or Okta show during authentication.

Similar techniques were used in vishing-based data theft attacks linked to the ShinyHunters extortion gang earlier this year to steal corporate single sign-on (SSO) credentials.

Once the process is completed, the phishing kit redirects victims to another website or displays a message thanking them for their application.

The researchers believe the phishing kit may have been developed using an AI coding assistant, based on hidden accessibility labels found in every analyzed version of the kit.

"The sign-in card in every build carries two hidden labels: "Sign-in simulation" and "GoodGuys sign-in simulation." They sit in accessibility text that only screen readers announce, so a visitor never sees them. We think the label is a leftover from how the kit was first built," explained Push Security.

### Remus malware steals Gmail messages via fake Google Sheets extension
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-26T15:23:28Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: active_exploitation, malware_botnet
- Summary: The Remus infostealer is stealing Gmail messages through a stealthy malicious Chrome extension, a capability the malware operators marketed as a zero-day exploit in Google's email service. [...]
- Technical details: The Remus infostealer is stealing Gmail messages through a stealthy malicious Chrome extension, a capability the malware operators marketed as a zero-day exploit in Google's email service.

On November 8, the Remus infostealer developer started advertising the alleged Gmail zero-day flaw that allowed the malware to collect emails even if victims changed their passwords and revoked all sessions.

Threat intelligence company Hudson Rock investigated the claims but came to inconclusive results. The researchers noted that evidence from the malware customers matched the seller's pitch, although there was no technical proof that a zero-day vulnerability was being exploited.

GenDigital and Invoke RE researchers published their independent findings yesterday, explaining that the advertised “zero-day” was just a malicious Chrome extension installed on the victims' browsers.

The extension is designed to steal unread emails from signed-in Gmail accounts. It can also survive password changes and session revocations because it remains installed in the victim's Chrome browser.

Its access to Gmail stops when the active session ends, and resumes again when the victim signs back in.

GenDigital discovered the extension after reverse-engineering the Remus infostealer. The researchers found no trace of an email-stealing module, suggesting that the functionality is implemented via an add-on.

“In a nutshell, the loaded module is a native DLL whose sole purpose is to install a malicious extension into Google Chrome, which, notably, is the only browser it targets,” GenDigital explains .

“The extension itself is bundled directly inside the DLL, stored as an unencrypted ZIP archive in its .rdata section.”

GenDigital explains that Chrome versions 137 and later removed a command-line option that allowed malware to load extensions.

Remus takes a complex route to overcome this restriction, editing the victim’s Chrome profile to register the malicious extension and then enabling ‘Developer Mode’ in the browser, which is required to run unpacked extensions.

The extension uses the name “Google Sheets,” copies the description of Google Docs Offline, and requests access to all websites.

Once active in the browser, it checks for unread emails every minute across up to 10 account slots and retrieves them in full, in raw format.

The extension sends the email subject, message body, sender, and victim’s email address to its command-and-control server, along with a campaign identifier.

Failed uploads are queued and retried, while a local record helps the extension keep track of what has succeeded so it doesn’t send duplicates.

Although the extension also parses attachment filenames, it does not send those to its operators, so this feature may not be fully implemented yet.

To defend against this threat, users are advised to download software only from trusted sources and scrutinize “chrome://extensions” for rogue installations or unexpected activation of Developer Mode.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### North Korea-Linked PolinRider Poisons Over 7,600 GitHub Repositories to Spread Malware
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-26T15:13:41Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: apt_intrusion, malware_botnet
- Summary: The threat actors behind the PolinRider campaign have compromised over 7,600 GitHub repositories, in some cases planting a Python loader capable of delivering next-stage payloads, as evidence emerges that the activity may have started as far back as February 2025. "These are real developers, not throwaway accounts," Apiiro researcher Shmulik Cohen said. "6,541 of the 7,609 repos (86%) belong
- Technical details: SafeDep found a PolinRider Python loader in 375 GitHub repositories that uses Ethereum transactions to retrieve and execute JavaScript.

The threat actors behind the PolinRider campaign have compromised over 7,600 GitHub repositories, in some cases planting a Python loader capable of delivering next-stage payloads, as evidence emerges that the activity may have started as far back as February 2025.

"These are real developers, not throwaway accounts," Apiiro researcher Shmulik Cohen said. "6,541 of the 7,609 repos (86%) belong to personal accounts more than a year old with five or more repos and real activity. Only 16 owners look like throwaways."

One of the latest updates to the campaign relates to deploying a Python loader on hundreds of compromised GitHub repositories, causing the malware to be propagated when unsuspecting developers clone or fork one of the affected projects, and run or import them.

"The loader hides in a small utils package or in conftest.py," SafeDep said in a report published this week. "When Python imports the file, the loader reads the newest transaction that an Ethereum wallet sent to itself. It decodes the input data of that transaction as JavaScript and runs it with Node.js."

The JavaScript code, for its part, reads a second wallet to locate the command-and-control (C2) server and then passes the next stage to a detached interactive Node.js environment by means of a node -i process.

The software supply chain security company said it identified the loader in 375 public repositories that belong to 260 accounts. There is no evidence that the loader has been distributed via PyPI or npm.

PolinRider is the name assigned to a malicious campaign linked to North Korean threat actors. Recent reports indicate that the activity employs various channels to distribute malware, including -

Fraudulent recruitment campaigns that make use of phishing emails and fake hiring portals like BlockNovas , AccuraSoftHire ("accurasofthire[.]com"), and HuntRole ("huntrole[.]org") to trick prospective targets into running malware-laced projects under the pretext of a take-home assessment.

Publishing malicious packages across open-source ecosystems by hijacking maintainer accounts to plant obfuscated malware through fake font files ( fa-solid-400.woff2 , fa-solid-500.woff2, or fa-solid-300.llf ), Visual Studio Code (VS Code) auto-run tasks, and Git history rewriting.

Using compromised developer machines to steal their GitHub tokens and force-push malicious commits into open-source repositories they have access to.

At a high level, the PolinRider loader retrieves an encrypted second-stage Node.js payload that scans the infected host for installed developer applications, such as GitHub Desktop, Cursor, VS Code, and Google Antigravity, and tampers with the most frequently used tools to execute attacker-controlled code and deliver infostealer malware.

It's worth noting here that PolinRider is assessed to be a sub-cluster operating in parallel with a long-running campaign codenamed Contagious Interview . While the latter is more of a social-engineering entry point that's used to compromise developer systems, PolinRider abuses the access to modify repositories and turn them into a distribution channel for malware.

"Cybercriminal groups deploy supply chain worms for opportunistic theft. On the other hand, North Korean state-sponsored threat actors use open-source software registries as a primary initial access vector targeting corporate environments," Palo Alto Networks Unit 42 said in a report published last month.

"Threat actors demonstrate how compromising a single maintainer account or registry scope converts open-source dependencies, resulting in widespread affected cloud environments."

Some of the decoy sites have been found to incorporate two other alternate delivery paths to distribute the loader -

Instruct candidates to sideload a Google Chrome extension after enabling "Developer mode" to subscribe to new job alerts.

Employ ClickFix-style lures to get victims to copy and paste malicious commands into the terminal app depending on the operating system as part of a "standard settings bundle for candidates and interview workflows."

"The [job] application asks for a short verification video," StepSecurity said . "Until the server marks a candidate as having installed a 'driver,' the camera check always fails with a message saying the system does not have a compatible camera environment."
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score
- [Open source item](https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/north-korea-linked-polinrider-poisons.html)

### USN-9212-1: PyJWT vulnerabilities
- Source: Ubuntu Security Notices | Published: 2026-11-26T14:45:08Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-102265, CVE-2026-103001 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: It was discovered that PyJWT incorrectly handled errors when parsing deeply nested token headers. An attacker could possibly use this issue to cause applications using PyJWT to fail unexpectedly, resulting in a denial of service. (CVE-2026-102265) It was discovered that PyJWT modified the options mapping supplied by an application when signature verification was disabled. If an application reused that mapping for a later call with signature verification enabled, an attacker could possibly use this issue to bypass token claim checks, such as expiration. This issue only affected Ubuntu 22.04 LTS. (CVE-2026-103001)
- Technical details: Ubuntu is an open source software operating system that runs from the desktop, to the cloud, to all your internet connected things.

It was discovered that PyJWT modified the options mapping supplied by an application when signature verification was disabled. If an application reused that mapping for a later call with signature verification enabled, an attacker could possibly use this issue to bypass token claim checks, such as expiration. This issue only affected Ubuntu 22.04 LTS. ( CVE-2026-103001 )

For details on how to apply this update, which includes the changes described in this advisory, refer to:

The system must be rebooted for this update to take effect.

Red Hat recommends treating all kernel errata as security-relevant. Given the kernel's fundamental role, any bug has a higher chance of impacting system security, even if that impact only becomes clear after a fix is published. Therefore, Red Hat prioritizes delivering fixes that improve our customers' overall security posture.

Because of this proactive approach, a patch may be associated with a CVE assignment at a future date. Retroactive CVE assignments are always documented in the corresponding errata and on Red Hat's CVE pages. We strongly advise against delaying updates, as doing so may leave your system exposed when protections are already available.

### USN-9211-1: Plexus Utils vulnerability
- Source: Ubuntu Security Notices | Published: 2026-11-26T13:25:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: It was discovered that Plexus Utils did not properly sanitize file paths when extracting archives. If a user or automated system were tricked into extracting a specially crafted archive, an attacker could possibly use this issue to write arbitrary files and execute arbitrary code.
- Technical details: Ubuntu is an open source software operating system that runs from the desktop, to the cloud, to all your internet connected things.

Thank you for contacting us. A member of our team will be in touch shortly. Close

Thank you for signing up for our newsletter! In these regular emails you will find the latest updates about Ubuntu and upcoming events where you can meet our team. Close

Your preferences have been successfully updated. Close notification

Plexus Utils could be made to overwrite files or run programs as your login if it opened a specially crafted archive.

It was discovered that Plexus Utils did not properly sanitize file paths when extracting archives. If a user or automated system were tricked into extracting a specially crafted archive, an attacker could possibly use this issue to write arbitrary files and execute arbitrary code.

In general, a standard system update will make all the necessary changes.

The problem can be corrected by updating your system to the following package versions:

Ubuntu Pro provides ten-year security coverage to 25,000+ packages in Main and Universe repositories, and it is free for up to five machines.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

### SYS010 malware abuses browser management to spy on Gmail, Outlook
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-26T11:50:17Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: malware_botnet
- Summary: A new version of the SYS01 infostealer malware delivered through Meta ads is enrolling browsers into enterprise-style management to force the installation of an extension that steals sensitive data, including Gmail and Outlook emails. [...]
- Technical details: A new version of the SYS01 infostealer malware delivered through Meta ads is enrolling browsers into enterprise-style management to force the installation of an extension that steals sensitive data, including Gmail and Outlook emails.

The ads lure Windows users into downloading free animated wallpapers that work as advertised but also deploy malware in the background during installation.

The campaign targets Facebook and Meta business accounts and gives the operator access to recent Gmail and Outlook messages in Chrome, and the ability to permanently delete emails.

Bitdefender Labs tracks the campaign as SYS010 and says the malware is likely a new version of the SYS01 infostealer , observed since at least 2022 in malvertising activity on Facebook .

The name reflects the researchers' assessment that this variant represents a major improvement over its predecessor, leading them to tag it as version 2, represented in binary as 010.

Researchers identified a malicious SYS010 ad on October 16 and found that the campaign was still ongoing, with hundreds of ads created.

Company telemetry shows that requests to the threat actor’s servers came from several countries in Asia, Europe, North America, Africa, and South America.

Meta Ad Library' transparency page shows that one advertisement alone reached 6,275 users in the European Union, targeting anyone above 18.

In a report today, Bitdefender says most requests came from the Philippines, Thailand, Bangladesh, Poland, Mexico, Algeria, Belgium, Mongolia, and the United States, but emphasizes these figures do not reflect confirmed infections.

The ads impersonate the legitimate Wallpaper Engine (a renamed, digitally signed version of the MeisterTask desktop app built on Electron) and Lively Wallpapers applications, directing users to malicious download sites, some of them using domains designed to appear official.

After installing the malicious Windows app, users get the advertised animated wallpaper functionality, which helps disguise the malware running in the background.

According to Bitdefender researchers, the attacker forces Chrome, Edge, and Firefox to install malicious browser add-ons by abusing management tools designed for organizations.

A script named eclient.php is responsible for installing the malicious extensions by modifying browser management policies.

In Chrome, it enrolls the browser in an attacker-controlled Google admin console, allowing forced installation of extensions.

In Firefox, it uses an ExtensionSettings policy to force-install an add-on while disabling Developer Tools and abuse reporting.

For Edge, it can enroll the victim's Windows device in an attacker-controlled mobile device management (MDM) server to enable forced extension installation.

These changes require administrator permissions, which is solved by triggering the User Account Control (UAC) prompt and repeating it every three seconds up to 99 times until the user approves the action.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score
