### Most Wanted: The Phishing Kits and Techniques Getting Past Security Controls
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-26T10:57:55Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: phishing, edge_vpn
- Summary: Every phishing attack has to get past email security, web filtering, and safe browsing before it reaches an employee. Some kits manage that far more often than others, and those are the kits that security teams need to know about. At Push Security, we measure this as breakthrough rate: The share of attacks that get past email security, web gateways, and safe browsing to reach the user. How an
- Technical details: Push Security reports DOMDIFFER led phishing kit breakthrough rates from August to October 2026, targeting corporate Google and Microsoft accounts.

Every phishing attack has to get past email security, web filtering, and safe browsing before it reaches an employee. Some kits manage that far more often than others, and those are the kits that security teams need to know about.

At Push Security, we measure this as breakthrough rate : The share of attacks that get past email security, web gateways, and safe browsing to reach the user.

Every browser-based attack starts with a lure, and each control in its path sees only part of the attack:

Email security: Half of the attacks we see never touch email. The lure arrives through a search ad, a chat message, social media, a calendar invite, or a QR code. The rest come from trusted senders and services, so they pass reputation checks.

Web gateways: The pages sit on trusted hosting and are assembled in the browser. The domains rotate every few days, so reputation data lags behind them.

Safe browsing and scanners: Bot checks and cloaking show scanners a harmless page. A new domain isn't on any blocklist yet.

When the page loads in the user's browser and the user interacts with it, that's a breakthrough. Breakthrough rate counts those attacks, not attempts.

This changes how to read upstream data. A kit with a high breakthrough rate is one that upstream controls are missing. A low count in email or proxy logs doesn't prove that a kit is rare, because the kit may be getting past those controls without a trace.

Our upcoming webinar reveals the five kits with the highest breakthrough rates. Here's an early look at the number one kit.

From August to October 2026, one kit ranked first by breakthrough rate every month. DOMDIFFER, an AiTM phishing kit that targets corporate Google and Microsoft accounts, broke through 2-4x as often as the next kit each month. DOM mirroring, the mechanism it uses under the hood, also happens to be the newest and fastest-growing way for a kit to relay a sign-in.

We first saw DOMDIFFER in February 2026. DOMDIFFER is our name for the kit (it hasn't yet been linked to a known phishing-as-a-service brand). We believe it grew out of NakedPages, a reverse proxy kit, and the two kits share infrastructure in several places. DOMDIFFER went quiet in the spring, came back over the summer, and has held the top rating since August. Most of its detections in that time came from fake calendar event lures.

One real DOMDIFFER attack starts with a fake Google Voice notification about a new voicemail.

The link passes a bot check that turns scanners away. The victim then sees what looks exactly like Google's sign-in page. It isn't a screenshot or a static copy. It's Google's real page, mirrored from the attacker's browser.

On the surface, this looks like fairly standard phishing: A notification lure, a bot check gate, then a sign-in page. What makes DOMDIFFER particularly effective lies beneath the surface.

AiTM kits relay a sign-in in one of four ways: Reverse proxy, replica page, pixel streaming, and DOM mirroring. In DOM mirroring, the attacker's own browser does the real sign-in. It sends the page's structure (the DOM) to the victim's browser, which rebuilds a working copy. Because a real browser does the sign-in, the kit sends Google genuine browser signals and handles new sign-in steps without needing extra code.

Replica pages are still the most common class, but DOM mirroring is the newest and the fastest growing in 2026. Laurseraph, the main criminal pixel-streaming kit, has switched to DOM mirroring, and kits such as BlueKit and PhantomDOM use it too.

Our research post, From reverse proxies to DOM mirroring , explains the four classes in detail.

DOM mirroring is only part of why DOMDIFFER breaks through security controls. The rest comes from how the kit reaches its victims and stays out of sight of the controls along the way. In the webinar on December 2, Luke will take apart DOMDIFFER and the other four kits in the top five. He'll show how each one works under the hood, and the tricks that get them past controls more often than other kits.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score
- [Open source item](https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/most-wanted-phishing-kits-and.html)

### 1Password Hacked, Customer Data and Source Code Stolen in GitHub Account Breach
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-26T10:57:25Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Popular password management software 1Password on Wednesday disclosed a security incident involving its backend systems that led to the unauthorized access of some customer information, but emphasized that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed. "While we took immediate steps to contain the incident, the attacker gained access to some of our backend systems and accessed a
- Technical details: 1Password says an attacker stole customer account data and source code via a compromised employee GitHub account, but vaults were unaffected.

Popular password management software 1Password on Wednesday disclosed a security incident involving its backend systems that led to the unauthorized access of some customer information, but emphasized that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.

"While we took immediate steps to contain the incident, the attacker gained access to some of our backend systems and accessed a limited amount of customer account information from a subset of our customers, including names and email addresses," the Toronto-based company said.

It noted that no customer vault content, including secrets, passwords, and other vault items, account recovery keys, or the keys that secure them, was accessed or compromised as a result of the breach. It also stated that customers do not need to take any action, but cautioned them to stay vigilant against unexpected phishing attempts.

As part of its efforts to remediate the incident, 1Password has disabled the affected GitHub account, revoked the stolen token, hardened GitHub, CI/CD, and endpoint controls, restored clean versions of the affected service, and rotated internal credentials. It has also lengthened the codes used in invitation and recovery emails from six digits to ten characters as a precautionary measure.

According to the company, the incident dates back to November 19, 2026, when it began investigating suspicious activity after an unknown threat actor gained access to an employee's corporate GitHub account.

The attacker is said to have weaponized the account to access source code and push malicious code to its backend services, resulting in the download of administrative data related to its customers, including -

Account and user identifiers, such as account creation and last sign-in times

Tokens present in certain emailed links for invitations, provisioning , and account recovery

Non-secret internal metadata about the account, such as version numeric identifiers, file numeric identifiers

1Password also noted that the tokens embedded in the links cannot be used on their own to gain access to customer accounts, as doing so requires either single sign-on (SSO) authentication or a six-digit verification code sent to the user's registered email address. However, it noted that a small number of users were affected and have been individually notified.

"The attacker also copied source code from private repositories and gained access to our CI/CD system," 1Password said. "Our source code is not customer data, and contains no user secrets. There is no impact to 1Password’s services, availability, or product security."

"Your vault secrets are encrypted with keys that only you hold, so we can’t access them, and neither can an attacker who gets into our servers."
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

Kaya Emre Arikan discovered that Guava did not correctly handle deserializing certain data. If a user or automated system were tricked into opening a specially crafted file, an attacker could possibly use this issue to cause a denial of service. ( CVE-2026-102554 )

In general, a standard system update will make all the necessary changes.

The problem can be corrected by updating your system to the following package versions:

Ubuntu Pro provides ten-year security coverage to 25,000+ packages in Main and Universe repositories, and it is free for up to five machines.

### Drupal Warns of New Highly Critical Site Takeover Vulnerability Under Active Exploitation
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-26T07:02:34Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-132165, CVE-2026-136716 | Topic signals: active_exploitation
- Summary: Drupal has warned of a new "highly critical" security flaw impacting Drupal core that it said has been exploited in the wild. The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-136716 (CVSS score: N/A), has been described as a case of improper checking for unusual or exceptional conditions. It also relates to CVE-2026-132165 aka Fail2Install in that it addresses remaining vulnerable installation states
- Technical details: Drupal patches an actively exploited core flaw that could allow site takeover on certain sites with remotely accessible installers.

Drupal has warned of a new "highly critical" security flaw impacting Drupal core that it said has been exploited in the wild.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-136716 (CVSS score: N/A), has been described as a case of improper checking for unusual or exceptional conditions. It also relates to CVE-2026-132165 aka Fail2Install in that it addresses remaining vulnerable installation states that could still be abused to facilitate site takeover even after the fix.

CVE-2026-132165 "recently fixed several vulnerabilities within the Drupal core installer," Drupal maintainers said . "However, certain sites in unusual or incomplete install states remain vulnerable to site takeover following that advisory, including production sites."

"It is not apparent in the user interface if a site is in the affected vulnerable state, and sites that are already compromised may no longer be in the vulnerable state."

Drupal acknowledged that the vulnerability is being exploited in the wild, but emphasized that site owners who have already secured the installer ("install.php") are not impacted.

Specifically, any website that left its installer remotely accessible following the fix for CVE-2026-132165 may have been compromised. Drupal is urging site administrators to take the following actions -

Check site logs for unauthorized access, including unexpected changes to or by the user 1 account

Ensure the user 1 email address is set to under their control, and consider resetting user 1's password.

The issue has been remediated in the following versions -

After updating to the latest supported versions, it's recommended to run "update.php" to fix existing production sites in this state and secure the Drupal installer on all new and existing sites.

According to statistics shared by StepSecurity, there are about 55,800 internet-facing Drupal sites as of November 26, 2026, out of which 31,500 services are running branches that are vulnerable to CVE-2026-132165 and roughly 15,000 are on the end-of-life 8 and 9 branches.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Hackers Exploit 10 Vulnerabilities Across Mitel, Juniper, Vates, and Danfoss Products

- Summary: The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) on Wednesday added 10 security flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, including five vulnerabilities impacting Mitel MiCollab that were disclosed last week. The Mitel MiCollab vulnerabilities are listed below - CVE-2026-133275 (CVSS score: 5.3) - An arbitrary file upload vulnerability in the Audio, Web and
- Technical details: CISA adds 10 exploited flaws to its KEV catalog, including five in Mitel MiCollab, and warns of an unpatched Danfoss controller flaw.

The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) on Wednesday added 10 security flaws to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities ( KEV ) catalog, including five vulnerabilities impacting Mitel MiCollab that were disclosed last week.

CVE-2026-133275 (CVSS score: 5.3) - An arbitrary file upload vulnerability in the Audio, Web and Video Conferencing (AWV) Component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to upload arbitrary files to the target system.

CVE-2026-133303 (CVSS score: 9.0) - An XML injection vulnerability in the Audio, Web and Video Conferencing (AWV) component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to execute arbitrary commands within the context of the system.

CVE-2026-133311 (CVSS score: 5.9) - An unauthorized access vulnerability in the Audio, Web, and Video Conferencing (AWV) Component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to obtain sensitive information.

CVE-2026-133323 (CVSS score: 10.0) - A command injection vulnerability in the Audio, Web, and Video Conferencing (AWV) Component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to execute arbitrary commands within the context of the system.

CVE-2026-133324 (CVSS score: 5.3) - An authentication bypass vulnerability in the Audio, Web, and Video Conferencing (AWV) Component of MiCollab that could allow an attacker to bypass authentication controls and perform unauthorized actions within the context of the application.

The issues affect MiCollab versions 10.3 and earlier. "Mitel provided a patch available for release 10.3 (10.3.0.18) that addresses these vulnerabilities," the company said in an advisory published November 16, 2026. "Upgrade to this release and apply the provided patch, or upgrade to a later release."

Customers who are unable to apply the updates are recommended to mitigate the risk by restricting external access to the AWV component. As of writing, there are currently no details on how the vulnerabilities are being exploited in the wild.

Customers who are unable to apply the updates are recommended to mitigate the risk by restricting external access to the AWV component. The addition comes against the backdrop of a report from Accenture's Apex Labs, which revealed that an unknown threat actor may have been weaponizing the flaws as zero-days as part of what's suspected to be an artificial intelligence (AI)-assisted data theft campaign.

Evidence indicates the deployment of a web shell against one victim organization a day before Mitel released patches, only for the intrusion to progress rapidly and allowing the threat actor to harvest credentials and steal sensitive business data from a line-of-business database server within hours.

"At that victim, we found the threat actor ran its first command 12 minutes after its first request, then reached root on the MiCollab server within 30 minutes and used the directory credentials that MiCollab stores for its LDAP connector to authenticate to a domain controller within 32 minutes," Accenture said.

The activity is assessed to be part of a broader campaign targeting MiCollab instances, with a second organization's MiCollab server observed holding a persistent HTTPS channel to the threat actor infrastructure since November 9, 2026. It has not been established how this server was compromised as it had been found running an older MiCollab version affected by previously disclosed flaws (i.e., MTLVULN-1694 ).

The indicators of compromise associated with the intrusions are listed below -

"MiCollab presents an attractive target because it is commonly internet-facing and connected to critical enterprise services, including Active Directory," Accenture said. "Its history of exploitation further underscores this risk."

Also added to the KEV catalog are the following vulnerabilities -

CVE-2026-133469 (CVSS score: 9.8) - An improper validation of specified type of input vulnerability in Vates Xen Orchestra that could allow an unauthenticated attacker to bypass authentication and perform actions with administrative privileges via a specially crafted authentication token containing an empty JSON object.

CVE-2026-103406 (CVSS score: 9.8) - An operating system command injection vulnerability in Hewlett Packard Enterprise (HPE) Juniper Session Smart Conductor and Session Smart Router that could allow a remote attacker to execute arbitrary commands as root via a specially crafted salt minion ID.

The addition of CVE-2026-133469 follows an advisory from Vates VMS that the vulnerability, also tracked as VSA-2026-062 , has been exploited by a threat actor from the following IP address and TOR domain -
- Fix / remediation: "Upgrade to this release and apply the provided patch, or upgrade to a later release." Upgrade to this release and apply the provided patch, or upgrade to a later release.

### 13 Malicious NuGet Packages Turn Fake .NET Job Assessments Into Malware Delivery Traps
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-26T03:48:52Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: malware_botnet
- Summary: Cybersecurity researchers have discovered 13 malicious NuGet packages that are designed to target Windows .NET developers under the guise of a fake take-home job assessment. "Most of the packages pose as WinUI 3 and WPF [Windows Presentation Foundation] button controls," Socket security researcher Kush Pandya said in a technical write-up. "In the versions we analyzed, building or running a
- Technical details: 13 malicious NuGet packages target Windows .NET developers through fake job assessments, using MSBuild and DLL sideloading to execute payloads.

Cybersecurity researchers have discovered 13 malicious NuGet packages that are designed to target Windows .NET developers under the guise of a fake take-home job assessment.

"Most of the packages pose as WinUI 3 and WPF [Windows Presentation Foundation] button controls," Socket security researcher Kush Pandya said in a technical write-up. "In the versions we analyzed, building or running a project that uses them downloads files from GitHub, stages a DLL sideloading pair around the Microsoft On-Screen Keyboard (osk.exe), or downloads and runs an executable."

All the identified NuGet packages have been deleted from the gallery as of writing. The names of the packages are listed below -

The first NuGet package, WinUI3IconButton, was uploaded to the registry on July 23, 2026, indicating that the campaign has been ongoing for quite some time without attracting attention. The last two uploads, HappyOne.MSBuildAnalyzer and HappyOne.WinUI3Controls, occurred on November 4, 2026, after both VS_Builder.MSBuildAnalyzer and VS_Builder.WinUI3Controls were removed by NuGet on November 2, 2026.

The starting point is a GitHub repository ("stevenleedig-ux/WinUI3_Home_Assessment") created on November 3, 2026. It features a set of assessment tasks revolving around enhancing a FileButton control and exception handling that candidates must implement within a span of three to four hours. It's unclear how targets are led to these decoy projects.

The README file also permits the test participants to use artificial intelligence (AI) development assistants like GitHub Copilot, ChatGPT, or Claude, but discourages them from copying and pasting the entire project into the tool and generating the solution without "understanding it."

Present within the assessment repository is a "WinUI3_Home_Assessment.csproj" file that references VS_Builder.MSBuildAnalyzer version 1.2.7 and VS_Builder.WinUI3Controls version 1.0.7.

"VS_Builder.MSBuildAnalyzer, later republished as HappyOne.MSBuildAnalyzer, ships an MSBuild targets file, build/VS_Builder.MsBuildAnalyzer.targets, that runs a bundled DLL ['MSBuildAnalyzer.dll'] after every build," Pandya said. "In the version bundled with the lure, that DLL downloads a file from GitHub to %LOCALAPPDATA%\osk\OskSupport.dll and copies C:\Windows\System32\osk.exe next to it."

The VS_Builder.WinUI3Controls package then launches the copied binary via the FileButton control that "routes clicks to Windows files and folders," triggering the execution of the DLL via a known technique called DLL sideloading .

In a similar vein, creating a WUIButton control from the WinUI.Button package starts a background routine in "lib/net8.0-windows10.0.19041/WinUI.Button.dll" that downloads "avast.exe" from the "Carol-White-Super/cloud" repository via the GitHub API using an embedded access token, saves it as "%TEMP%\Launcher.exe," and runs it.

RevertButtonIcon and SetButtonColor "download a Windows executable to the user's temp folder and run it, and InitializeCustomButton starts the copy that SetButtonColor drops," Socket explained.

"Five more are tied to the set by dependency or by the lure: WinUI3IconButton (from version 1.1.4), StyledAppButton and StyledComponent depend on other packages in the set, WindowsAccentColorName is a dependency of SetButtonColor and InitializeCustomButton, and HappyOne.WinUI3Controls is the FileButton package the lure switched to."

Socket also said it identified two bogus repositories impersonating the axios JavaScript library -

stevenleedig-ux/axios-http (the same GitHub account hosting the assessment)

Both repositories have been flagged as copies of a malicious Node.js library named "axios-test-one" that impersonates the Axios HTTP client but contains functionality that enables code execution and shell access. The repositories serve a Windows executable named "lib/env/axios.csproj."

An analysis of their commit history has revealed several binaries between the two repositories, including -

A loader ("updater.exe" and "msedgewebview2.exe") that uses a GitHub repository ("James-White-Super/React_prj") as a two-way command and control (C2)

A PyInstaller program that functions as a Google Chrome profile stealer and uploads the information to GitHub ("gabsantosbus-star/EngineeringRepository"), and
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Palo Alto Networks warns of Fragnesia flaw exploited in PAN-OS attacks
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-25T22:05:09Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-109618, CVE-2026-109619, CVE-2026-46300 | Topic signals: apt_intrusion
- Summary: Threat actors are chaining exploits for the PHP2Shell and Fragnesia vulnerabilities to target Palo Alto Networks devices with management interfaces exposed to the internet. [...]
- Technical details: Threat actors are chaining exploits for the PHP2Shell and Fragnesia vulnerabilities to target Palo Alto Networks devices with management interfaces exposed to the internet.

PHP2Shell is the name for two critical PHP vulnerabilities tracked as CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619, which allow unauthenticated attackers to run code remotely with the privileges of the PHP web worker.

The Fragnesia vulnerability , identified as CVE-2026-46300, is a Linux kernel vulnerability in the XFRM ESP-in-TCP subsystem that lets a local attacker with low-privileges to escalate permissions to root level.

An alert from CISA on October 28 warned that both security issues were being chained in attacks to deploy PHP webshells on vulnerable systems and execute commands as root.

Around the same time, Dream Security researchers reported observing a campaign using the same chain, targeting servers in the United States, Japan, Taiwan, and South Korea, including a U.S. government affiliate.

Palo Alto Networks has confirmed in an advisory today that such attacks also target firewalls running PAN-OS.

“Exploitation of CVE-2026-46300 has been observed targeting the Palo Alto Networks management interface exposed to the internet,” reads the security bulletin .

The networking company says that all exploitation activity it observed involved PAN-OS devices that exposed their management interface to the public internet, which goes against its recommended ‘ best practices .’

Palo Alto Networks did not provide any technical details about the chaining of the PHP2Shell vulnerabilities, but noted that this is the only known way to exploit Fragnesia, and they are not aware of another exploitation pathway for the flaw.

The company has released fixes for Fragnesia for PAN-OS 10.2, 11.1, 11.2, 12.1, and 12.2, with the exact patch-carrying build varying by release.

Given that the two flaws are exploited together, administrators who applied updates that addressed the PHP2Shell flaw should be safe from these attacks.

In addition to applying the security updates, Palo Alto Networks also recommends that those who need to keep a remote management interface available restrict access to trusted internal IP addresses.

The OSS CVEs are fixed in the respective PAN-OS versions listed in the above table.

### 1Password customer data stolen after employee GitHub account breach
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-25T21:33:33Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: 1Password has disclosed a data breach after a an employee's corporate GitHub account was hacked and used to deploy malicious code to the company's backend services to steal customer data. [...]
- Technical details: 1Password has disclosed a data breach after a an employee's corporate GitHub account was hacked and used to deploy malicious code to the company's backend services to steal customer data.

1Password has disclosed a data breach after an employee's corporate GitHub account was hacked and used to deploy malicious code to the company's backend services to steal customer data.

The company says customer password vaults and the encryption keys protecting them were not compromised in the attack.

In a security incident disclosure published today, 1Password says it began investigating the breach on November 19 after detecting indications that an employee's GitHub account had been compromised.

The attacker used the account to access private source code repositories and inject malicious code into backend services that downloaded customer account records.

The attacker also stole internal company source code and accessed its continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) environment, potentially exposing internal infrastructure credentials.

Among the stolen customer data were tokens embedded in links that 1Password emailed to users to accept account provisioning invitations or recover their accounts.

However, 1Password says these tokens cannot be used on their own to access customer accounts, as completing the invitation or recovery process requires either single sign-on (SSO) authentication or a six-digit verification code sent to the user's registered email address.

The company says only a small number of users were affected and have been individually notified.

As a precaution against brute-force attacks, 1Password has strengthened these verification codes from six numeric digits to ten alphanumeric characters.

The company says it has now disabled the compromised GitHub account, revoked the stolen token, restored clean versions of affected services, and rotated or revoked potentially exposed infrastructure credentials.

1Password has also hired independent cybersecurity experts to validate its remediation efforts and says it has found no internal evidence of customer account takeovers.

The company is not requiring customers to change their account passwords or Secret Keys but warns them to watch for unexpected invitations, account recovery requests, and other potential phishing attempts.

While customer vaults were not compromised in the attack, the stolen data is valuable to threat actors as it provides a list of known 1Password users who could be targeted in phishing campaigns.

Threat actors could send emails impersonating 1Password, using the breach as a lure to trick customers into disclosing their account passwords, Secret Keys, or recovery verification codes.

Customers should be particularly wary of emails claiming they need to secure their accounts in response to the breach, reset their passwords, or verify their account information.

Rather than clicking on links in these emails, customers should access their accounts directly through the 1Password website or app and never share their passwords, Secret Keys, or verification codes with anyone.

BleepingComputer contacted 1Password with questions about the attack but has not yet received a response.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

This advisory is available at the following link: https://sec.cloudapps.cisco.com/security/center/content/CiscoSecurityAdvisory/cisco-sa-emudhra-info-ScS22A1r

Cisco has investigated its product line to determine which products may be impacted by the eMudhra Intermediate CA Compromise.

The following table lists Cisco products that are impacted by the eMudhra compromise that is described in this advisory. For impacted products, Cisco will provide Cisco remediation steps, product release notes, or information about new software that may be released.

Cisco has investigated its product line to determine which products may be affected by this issue. Cisco has confirmed that this issue does not affect the following Cisco products:

Anyone in possession of these weak private keys can issue fraudulent TLS/HTTPS certificates for any domain on the internet. The eight compromised Issuing CAs include:

6 TLS Issuing CAs: Covering Domain Validated (DV), Organization Validated (OV), and Extended Validation (EV).

2 Code Signing CAs: Allowing for the signing of malicious software that appears to come from a trusted source.

Critically , all eight certificates were cross-signed by Sectigo’s AAA Certificate Services root in May 2025. This cross-signature ensured that the compromised keys were trusted by almost every device, browser, and operating system globally, even if those systems did not natively trust the eMudhra roots.

This issue impacts any Cisco product that trusts these compromised eMudhra intermediate certificates, regardless of whether the trust is managed through Cisco's Trusted Root Store (TRS) bundles, embedded certificates, or other third-party trust mechanisms.

Any workarounds will be documented in the Vulnerable Products section of this advisory.

The Cisco Product Security Incident Response Team (PSIRT) is aware that a public announcement is available for the issue that is described in this advisory.

The Cisco PSIRT is not aware of any malicious use of the issue that is described in this advisory.

Cisco would like to thank eMudhra, who reported this issue.

To learn about Cisco security vulnerability disclosure policies and publications, see the Security Vulnerability Policy . This document also contains instructions for obtaining fixed software and receiving security vulnerability information from Cisco.

### CVE-2026-134070 - Improper input sanitization in the AWS Config remediation runbook AWSConfigRemediation-EnableSystemsManagerSessionManagerAuditLogsToS3
- Source: AWS Security Bulletins | Published: 2026-11-25T19:41:20Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-134070 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Bulletin ID: 2026-203-AWS Publication Date: 11/25/2026 11:30 AM PST Description: AWS Systems Manager Automation provides AWS-managed runbooks that customers can run directly or through integrations such as AWS Config remediation. Session Manager, a capability of AWS Systems Manager, stores each account's regional session preferences in the SSM-SessionManagerRunShell document. We identified CVE-2026-134070, where improper input validation in the AWS-managed AWS Systems Manager Automation runbook AWSConfigRemediation-EnableSystemsManagerSessionManagerAuditLogsToS3 might allow an authenticated remote user who is permitted to run the runbook to set unintended configuration in the account's Regional Session Manager preferences document (SSM-SessionManagerRunShell) via a crafted S3KeyPrefix parameter value. This applies only when that document does not already exist in the Region. The unintend
- Technical details: Bulletin ID: 2026-203-AWS Scope: AWS Content Type: Important (requires attention) Publication Date: 11/25/2026 11:30 AM PST

AWS Systems Manager Automation provides AWS-managed runbooks that customers can run directly or through integrations such as AWS Config remediation. Session Manager, a capability of AWS Systems Manager, stores each account's regional session preferences in the SSM-SessionManagerRunShell document.

We identified CVE-2026-134070 , where improper input validation in the AWS-managed AWS Systems Manager Automation runbook AWSConfigRemediation-EnableSystemsManagerSessionManagerAuditLogsToS3 might allow an authenticated remote user who is permitted to run the runbook to set unintended configuration in the account's Regional Session Manager preferences document (SSM-SessionManagerRunShell) via a crafted S3KeyPrefix parameter value. This applies only when that document does not already exist in the Region. The unintended configuration can include arbitrary commands, which then run on managed instances when other users start Session Manager sessions.

Impacted versions: Revisions created before October 22, 2026 (UTC)

This issue has been addressed in a fixed revision of the AWS Systems Manager Automation runbook AWSConfigRemediation-EnableSystemsManagerSessionManagerAuditLogsToS3, available in all Regions where the runbook is available. The fixed revision is the latest revision in each Region. Its revision number varies by Region. To find it, run: aws ssm describe-document --name AWSConfigRemediation-EnableSystemsManagerSessionManagerAuditLogsToS3 --region <region> --query "Document.LatestVersion" We recommend upgrading to the latest revision and ensuring any forked or derivative code is patched to incorporate the new fixes. Update any Automation executions, State Manager associations, AWS Config remediation configurations, or other automations pinned to an earlier revision to use the latest revision.

Any user permitted to run the runbook can choose which revision to run. Earlier revisions remain runnable until AWS deprecates all revisions created before October 22, 2026 (UTC), beginning January 15, 2027, in every Region and partition. Until then, follow the protection described under Workarounds, and confirm that all invocation paths use the latest revision.

The fixed runbook updates only the S3 bucket and prefix fields of an existing Session Manager preferences document, so any settings injected before you updated remain in place. You need to review a Region only if your Session Manager preferences document (SSM-SessionManagerRunShell) was first created there by an earlier revision of this runbook, not by you. To check: Look for any run of this runbook that failed with "DOCUMENT CREATE OR UPDATE FAILED." An injected run always fails with this error. Find when the document was first created by running aws ssm list-document-versions --name SSM-SessionManagerRunShell --region <region> and noting the CreatedDate of the first version. If that date matches a run of this runbook, the runbook created the document. To view the current settings, run aws ssm get-document --name SSM-SessionManagerRunShell --region <region> --query Content --output text. Repeat this check in each Region where you use Session Manager. Look for settings you did not configure, especially: shellProfile: commands that run at the start of every session, runAsEnabled and runAsDefaultUser: the operating system user that sessions run as, maxSessionDuration and idleSessionTimeout, the logging destination fields (s3BucketName, s3KeyPrefix, cloudWatchLogGroupName, kmsKeyId).

If you find any, restore your trusted preferences and review instances on which Session Manager sessions were started while those settings were active.

Until AWS removes earlier revisions in your Region, do one of the following:

Keep untrusted users from running earlier revisions of the runbook.

Keep a trusted Session Manager preferences document (SSM-SessionManagerRunShell) in every Region. Earlier revisions inject only when they create this document, so while a trusted one exists, this path is closed in that Region.

Keep this protection in place until earlier revisions are removed. Also complete the review described under "Review your Session Manager preferences".

We would like to thank k4ddy for collaborating on this issue through the coordinated vulnerability disclosure process.

Please email aws-security@amazon.com with any s ecurity questions or concerns.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: CVE-2026-134070 0.006 (0.6% next-30-day estimate), percentile 0.402, dated 2026-11-26 — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

### Italy and Switzerland warn of autonomous AI-driven cyberattacks
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-25T19:39:31Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: Swiss and Italian cybersecurity agencies are warning about automated cyberattacks powered by AI agents targeting organizations in the critical infrastructure sector. [...]
- Technical details: Swiss and Italian cybersecurity agencies are warning about automated cyberattacks powered by AI agents targeting organizations in the critical infrastructure sector.

The two agencies listed four AI-powered penetration testing and red-teaming frameworks that have been involved in recent breaches and hacking incidents : ARTEX AI, CyberStrikeAI, DeepSeek Harness - Red Team, and PentAGI.

Both agencies have urged organizations to reduce the number of systems exposed on the internet, prioritize patching, and monitor for unusual activity.

CSIRT Italia says it detected large-scale malicious campaigns that used autonomous AI agents to identify and exploit vulnerabilities and misconfigurations on exposed endpoints.

In some cases, the agency observed multiple AI agents working in parallel to compromise a large number of systems within a few hours.

In one instance involving a DeepSeek Harness setup, a coordinator assigned tasks to five AI agents that undertook a broad spectrum of operational stages, from information gathering to exploitation and persistence.

The agents chained vulnerabilities, misconfigurations, and compromised credentials, leveraging exposed systems as a foothold to reach internal networks.

“During the observed campaigns, autonomous agents demonstrated the ability to carry out complex, sustained activity to detect misconfigurations, link even low-impact vulnerabilities into complex attack chains, and dynamically adapt their operating modules,” CSIRT Italia says .

“They went as far as writing malicious code in real time to evade endpoint defenses and carry out actions deeper within the infrastructure.”

The Italian cybersecurity agency advises administrators to prioritize patching internet-facing systems, focus on addressing flaws known to be actively exploited in the wild, and disable remote management interfaces if not needed.

The agency also recommends monitoring control plane operations, authentication sources, traffic to internal networks, and outbound server traffic to detect suspicious activity.

Switzerland’s National Cyber Security Center (NCSC) warned of similar campaigns targeting organizations in the country, confirming the first cases of successful AI-assisted cyberattacks.

“In recent days, the NCSC has received its first reports of cyberattacks against organisations in Switzerland in which the attack was carried out with the assistance of an AI agent,” the Swiss NCSC said .

“Various organisations and operators of critical infrastructure across at least two sectors have been affected by these cyberattacks.”

“In all cyberattacks known to the NCSC, the use of AI agents led to the successful compromise of the victim’s IT systems.”

The agency described rapid attacks in which agents exploited weak passwords and known vulnerabilities in exposed endpoints, using frameworks to verify weaknesses and independently decide what to do next.

NCSC says that the number of AI agents publicly (and often freely) available to cybercriminals has nearly doubled since October, resulting in these massive waves against Swiss organizations, including critical infrastructure operators.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Oh, is it Thanksgiving? I hadn’t noticed!
- Source: Cisco Talos | Published: 2026-11-25T19:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: apt_intrusion
- Summary: In true Thanksgiving fashion, David has a few thank-yous to give to the cybersecurity community (and a few choice words for threat actors).
- Technical details: In true Thanksgiving fashion, David has a few thank-yous to give to the cybersecurity community (and a few choice words for threat actors).

I tried hard not to write another typical “Thanksgiving article.” Obviously, I failed. But you know what? It is Thanksgiving week here in the U.S., and coming up with fun, interesting, short form topics is hard , especially when you’re fighting the weight of Tradition expected this week. So instead of me writing something insightful, you’re getting some things I’m thankful for. Even that’s harder than you might think, because there are so many things, and this is just the newsletter introduction , after all.

Let’s start off with something I’m pretty sure none of us are thankful for: threat actors. Seriously, screw you guys! I know I'd be out of a job without you, but that’s just another way of saying “I coulda been a professional bagpiper.” There are days when that doesn’t sound so bad. So, no gratitude coming your way, people!

In terms of actual thankfulness, I want to give a big THANK YOU to the entire Blue Team, everywhere. Everyone out there defending, especially those in the U.S. taking shifts on Thursday to keep the turkey-eating public safe and secure. This is a hard, often high-pressure job for which you get little thanks, but all the blame when a breach occurs. We see you. We appreciate you. We promise we’ll save you some stuffing.

Speaking of defenders, I’m also very thankful to get to work with so many talented and wonderful people here at Talos. I’m not just saying this because I know my boss will read it! Without exception, everyone I’ve worked with here has not only been an expert at what they do, but they’ve been friendly, welcoming, and very patient with me as I’ve been feeling my way around this new org over the past several months. Thank you!

Finally, and maybe most importantly of all, I want to thank you , our readers. You are the reason we’re all here, doing what we do, helping you to look out for your own networks and protect your own people. We appreciate your trust!

Whether you’re celebrating Thanksgiving here in the States or just having a normal weekend wherever you are, I hope you get some time to relax, unplug, recharge, and de-stress.

In Talos’ latest blog , we examine the fascinating tradecraft of the OpenAI-linked agentic swarm that broke out of its containerized environment in 2026. While not inherently malicious, this swarm of AI agents used public internet services as improvised infrastructure to bypass their sandbox constraints and complete their training tasks. By analyzing this unintentional great escape, we can learn how multi-agent systems coordinate, partition work, and adapt when their paths are blocked. This view into autonomous AI operations provides a baseline for understanding how these behaviors transfer to purpose-built cyber attack frameworks.

The leap from an AI sandbox escape to weaponized cyber attacks has already happened. Agentic frameworks like ARTEX are currently in the wild, proving that multi-agent systems can move from a single compromised credential to deep network access in under 15 minutes. These autonomous threats adapt to errors in real time and execute dozens of actions in mere seconds. Understanding how these swarms coordinate, adapt, and exfiltrate data is a necessity to keep up with an adversary that operates at machine speed.

To catch an AI swarm, defenders must look for its operational fingerprints. Monitor your environment for irregular "burst-and-pause" timing patterns that indicate rapid, sub-second automated reasoning. Keep a close eye on unusual traffic to allow-listed public services like “webhook.site”, which agents use to park data before terminating. Finally, hunt for self-labeling conventions, like highly specific URL parameters or predictable file names (e.g., “artex-probe-*”), that agents rely on to track their state. Read the full blog for a deeper dive into these swarm behaviors.

AI-assisted cyber attacks trigger unauthorized payments at Indian banks and fintech firms The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) warned the attacks have resulted in unauthorized financial transactions at banking and financial services entities, as well as technology service providers supporting government-linked payments and platforms handling vendor or partner payouts. ( The Hacker News )

JPMorgan, Visa confirm customer data taken in third-party vendor hacks Marketing data, including email addresses, were affected. Both Visa and JPMorgan said the incidents were unrelated to tech disruptions reported earlier this week. ( Cybersecurity Dive )

OpenAI and Cisco unveil AI-assisted Linux security tool to block exploits before patching OpenAI and Cisco have teamed up to launch Shirudo (meaning "shield" in Japanese), an open-source Linux runtime that allows AI agents to defend systems without downtime. ( The Hacker News )

AI-enabled campaign targets Check Point Security Management servers The attacks led to at least 22 organizations being compromised across the globe, including in the UK, the U.S., France, Germany, Australia, China, India and Australia. The targeted organizations work in the energy, transportation, financial services and government sectors. ( Cybersecurity Dive )

Drupal Fail2Install exploit details emerge as critical RCE flaw faces active exploitation While Drupal has checks in place to determine whether the website is already installed before allowing installation to proceed, the research found that it's possible to bypass these checks by exhausting server resources. ( The Hacker News )

Beers with Talos: Ranksgiving with guest Anton Chuvakin This Ranksgiving, Dave has a brand new (though no less controversial) topic: the top five kitchen utensils/gadgets needed to make Thanksgiving dinner. Prepare to be both aghast and disappointed (as usual) in the team's choices.

UAT-12643 blends blockchain resilience with targeted infostealing Talos discovered UAT-12643 using Overlord RAT, which implements a command-and-control (C2) resilience technique in which encrypted server URLs are embedded in Solana blockchain transaction memos.

- Technical details: We are proud to share that Rapid7 has been recognized as a Leader in the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Integrated Security Operations Center Systems. The Gartner Magic Quadrant evaluates vendors based on their Ability to Execute and Completeness of Vision, and we are honored to be included among the recognized vendors in this important report.

Recognized for Completeness of Vision and Ability to Execute.

We are proud to share that Rapid7 has been recognized as a Leader in the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Integrated Security Operations Center Systems.

Most security teams don't struggle to find tools. They struggle to make them work together. We believe SOC analysts lose too much of their day moving between consoles and matching alerts to assets by hand, which is time not spent stopping threats.

To us, an integrated approach to security operations means detection, investigation and response working from the same data and the same view, so analysts can get from "what fired?" to "what do we do about it?" faster.

Rapid7 Incident Command, part of the Rapid7 Command Platform, brings together SIEM, SOAR, DFIR, attack surface management and threat intelligence. We built it on the idea that exposure management, and the context it brings, belongs inside the SOC, not next to it. In our view, when an alert arrives with what we know about the asset and how exposed it is, analysts can prioritize what matters instead of working from separate vulnerability reports and alert queues.

We believe adopting an integrated SOC shouldn't be all-or-nothing. Incident Command is available in tiers from Essentials to Ultimate, so teams that want extra coverage can add managed services. That helps resource-pressured teams to start with what they need and grow at their own pace, spending less time building and maintaining integrations and more time on investigations.

To us, security operations work best when analysts have the context to act, not another console to check. We're proud to be recognized as a Leader in this Magic Quadrant, and we believe it reflects our focus on bringing detection, response and exposure context together for our customers. ⠀ The Gartner Magic Quadrant evaluates vendors based on their Ability to Execute and Completeness of Vision. We are honored to be included among the recognized vendors in this important report. Learn more about the Gartner Magic Quadrant here .

Gartner, Magic Quadrant for Integrated Security Operations Center Systems, Angel Berrios, Kevin Schmidt, Eric Ahlm, 24 November 2026.

Gartner does not endorse any vendor, product or service depicted in its research publications, and does not advise technology users to select only those vendors with the highest ratings or other designation. Gartner research publications consist of the opinions of Gartner’s research organization and should not be construed as statements of fact. Gartner disclaims all warranties, expressed or implied, with respect to this research, including any warranties of merchantability or fitness for a particular purpose.

GARTNER is a registered trademark and service mark of Gartner, Inc. and/or its affiliates in the U.S. and internationally. MAGIC QUADRANT is a registered trademark of Gartner, Inc. and/or its affiliates and is used herein with permission. All rights reserved.

### Cisco UCS and UCS-Based Appliances UEFI Shell Secure Boot Bypass Vulnerability
- Source: Cisco PSIRT | Published: 2026-11-25T18:29:45Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2024-20356, CVE-2026-20293 | Topic signals: identity
- Summary: A vulnerability in the Unified Extensible Firmware Interface (UEFI) Shell implementation of Cisco UCS Servers and UCS-based appliances could allow an authenticated attacker with valid credentials for a user account with the role of user or admin or an unauthenticated attacker with physical access to an affected device to bypass UEFI Secure Boot validation checks and execute unauthorized software. This vulnerability is due to the availability of memory write commands in the UEFI Shell while UEFI Secure Boot is enabled on a device. An attacker could exploit this vulnerability by selecting the UEFI Shell boot option at boot time and using available shell commands to modify UEFI memory variables. A successful exploit could allow the attacker to manipulate the preboot environment, overwrite UEFI Secure Boot-related memory values, and execute unauthorized software on the affected device. Cisco
- Technical details: A vulnerability in the Unified Extensible Firmware Interface (UEFI) Shell implementation of Cisco UCS Servers and UCS-based appliances could allow an authenticated attacker with valid credentials for a user account with the role of user or admin or an unauthenticated attacker with physical access to an affected device to bypass UEFI Secure Boot validation checks and execute unauthorized software. This vulnerability is due to the availability of memory write commands in the UEFI Shell while UEFI Secure Boot is enabled on a device. An attacker could exploit this vulnerability by selecting the UEFI Shell boot option at boot time and using available shell commands to modify UEFI memory variables. A successful exploit could allow the attacker to manipulate the preboot environment, overwrite UEFI Secure Boot-related memory values, and execute unauthorized software on the affected device. Cisco has released software updates that address this vulnerability. There are no workarounds that address this vulnerability. This advisory is available at the following link:https://sec.cloudapps.cisco.com/security/center/content/CiscoSecurityAdvisory/cisco-sa-ucs-uefi-sb-bypass-eb6xC5GW

A vulnerability in the Unified Extensible Firmware Interface (UEFI) Shell implementation of Cisco UCS Servers and UCS-based appliances could allow an authenticated attacker with valid credentials for a user account with the role of user or admin or an unauthenticated attacker with physical access to an affected device to bypass UEFI Secure Boot validation checks and execute unauthorized software.

This vulnerability is due to the availability of memory write commands in the UEFI Shell while UEFI Secure Boot is enabled on a device. An attacker could exploit this vulnerability by selecting the UEFI Shell boot option at boot time and using available shell commands to modify UEFI memory variables. A successful exploit could allow the attacker to manipulate the preboot environment, overwrite UEFI Secure Boot-related memory values, and execute unauthorized software on the affected device.

Cisco has released software updates that address this vulnerability. There are no workarounds that address this vulnerability.

This advisory is available at the following link: https://sec.cloudapps.cisco.com/security/center/content/CiscoSecurityAdvisory/cisco-sa-ucs-uefi-sb-bypass-eb6xC5GW

This vulnerability affects the following Cisco products if they have UEFI Secure Boot enabled and are running a vulnerable BIOS version:

5000 Series Enterprise Network Compute Systems (ENCS) ( CSCwu42927 )

Cisco appliances that are based on a preconfigured version of a Cisco UCS C-Series Server are also affected by this vulnerability if they support UEFI Secure Boot and expose access to the keyboard, video, and mouse (KVM) or virtual KVM console. At the time of publication, this included the following Cisco products:

Application Policy Infrastructure Controller (APIC) Servers

1. Physical KVM access is required to exploit the vulnerability on these appliances, which significantly reduces the attack vector on these platforms.

For information about which Cisco software releases are vulnerable, see the Fixed Software section of this advisory.

Only products listed in the Vulnerable Products section of this advisory are known to be affected by this vulnerability.

The UEFI Shell is a command line interface that provides commands to debug, script, and control various booting options. It is available as a boot option on Cisco UCS Servers that are in UEFI boot mode.

The UEFI Shell includes multiple commands that allow memory modifications. When UEFI Secure Boot is enabled on a device, the availability of such commands could allow an attacker to manipulate the preboot environment, including the possibility of overwriting UEFI Secure Boot-related memory values, effectively bypassing UEFI Secure Boot validations.

As a resolution for this vulnerability, UEFI Shell commands that allow memory modifications are removed from the shell when UEFI Secure Boot is enabled on a device.

There are no workarounds that address this vulnerability.

Cisco considers any workarounds and mitigations (if applicable) to be temporary solutions until an upgrade to a fixed software release is available. To remediate this vulnerability and avoid future exposure as described in this advisory, Cisco strongly recommends that customers upgrade to the fixed software indicated in this advisory.

In the following tables, the left column lists Cisco software releases. The right column indicates whether a release is affected by the vulnerability that is described in this advisory and the first release that includes the fix for this vulnerability. Customers are advised to upgrade to an appropriate fixed software release as indicated in this section.

Note: Upgrading Cisco Integrated Management Controller (IMC) and BIOS on Cisco 5000 Series ENCS requires upgrading Cisco Enterprise NFV Infrastructure Software (NFVIS) on the platforms. Cisco IMC and BIOS are upgraded as part of the firmware auto-upgrade process.
- Fix / remediation: Remediation of CVE-2024-20356 requires an update to the CIMC firmware for the Cisco Secure Endpoint Private Cloud appliance.

### CVE-2026-136267 - Improper neutralization of special elements used in an OS command in Kiro Web
- Source: AWS Security Bulletins | Published: 2026-11-25T17:08:36Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-136267 | Topic signals: cloud, ai_threat
- Summary: Bulletin ID: 2026-202-AWS Publication Date: 11/25/2026 09:00 AM PST Description: Kiro Web is a cloud-hosted, AI-powered integrated development environment where subscribers use AI agents to work on software development tasks in the browser. We are aware of CVE-2026-136267, an issue in Kiro Web where tool-call markup inside content an agent session read, such as a repository file or a web page, could be repeated by certain models and run as a command in the subscriber's hosted development environment without a consent prompt. The issue affected agent sessions using DeepSeek v3.2, GLM-5, MiniMax M2.1, MiniMax M2.5, or Qwen3-coder-next from 2026-05-18 through 2026-10-30. Impacted versions: Kiro Web before 2026-10-30 Refer to the article for the most up-to-date and complete information.
- Technical details: Bulletin ID: 2026-202-AWS Scope: AWS Content Type: Important (requires attention) Publication Date: 11/25/2026 09:00 AM PST

Kiro Web is a cloud-hosted, AI-powered integrated development environment where subscribers use AI agents to work on software development tasks in the browser.

We are aware of CVE-2026-136267 , an issue in Kiro Web where tool-call markup inside content an agent session read, such as a repository file or a web page, could be repeated by certain models and run as a command in the subscriber's hosted development environment without a consent prompt. The issue affected agent sessions using DeepSeek v3.2, GLM-5, MiniMax M2.1, MiniMax M2.5, or Qwen3-coder-next from 2026-05-18 through 2026-10-30.

This issue was addressed in Kiro Web through a service-side update completed on October 30, 2026. No customer action is required.

No workaround is required. The issue has been addressed service-side.

We would like to thank Brad Rucinski for collaborating on this issue through the coordinated vulnerability disclosure process.

Please email aws-security@amazon.com with any s ecurity questions or concerns.

### The AI Threat Horizon: Why Speed, Edge AI and Baselines Are Our Best Defense
- Source: Palo Alto Unit 42 | Published: 2026-11-25T15:00:09Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: Unit 42 examines the evolution of AI-driven cyberattacks and outlines proactive defense strategies for security teams. The post The AI Threat Horizon: Why Speed, Edge AI and Baselines Are Our Best Defense appeared first on Unit 42 .
- Technical details: Unit 42 examines the evolution of AI-driven cyberattacks and outlines proactive defense strategies for security teams.

AI-driven attacks are on the rise, transitioning from theoretical risks to real-world threats. Recent investigations reveal that criminals are using AI to accelerate attacks at scale. We have observed attackers leveraging AI agents to execute context-aware commands with real-time decision-making . In one campaign that we observed, a highly persistent threat actor utilizing AI generated 117 unique scripts across multiple languages and executed over 8,000 unique commands in just 12 days whereas normal attackers usually do dozens of commands with a few scripts for similar time periods.

The behavioral difference between human operators and AI agents is staggering. In one attack we observed, humans acted followed by an agentic AI attacker. In the first (human attacker) phase, time between commands averaged roughly 24 seconds. The hackers made typographical errors, repeated manual checks and demonstrated environment confusion. On the other hand, in the agentic phase of the attack, we observed flawless, zero-syntax-error commands with a median gap of just 1.4 seconds.

When an AI agent is instructed to execute aspects of an attack, it rapidly scans networks, attempts to escalate privileges and utilizes legitimate native tools to blend in. Because of AI’s machine-speed execution, our Mean Time To Respond (MTTR) metrics must improve considerably. Attack isolation and near real-time response are now critical mandates. If we wait hours to react, an AI agent can have already enumerated our cloud infrastructure and extracted or encrypted our data.

To effectively defend against AI-powered attacks, defenders must employ AI-powered tools. This should be done right where the attacks happen. Defenders should also strive to understand the behaviors that separate AI-based attacks from normal operations, whether fully autonomous or partially controlled by a human.

To counter adversaries operating at automated speeds, defense must occur where the attack takes place. Leveraging AI on the edge, specifically on endpoints and Next-Generation Firewalls (NGFW), is a major strategic necessity. Edge-based AI can process telemetry instantly, making split-second decisions to terminate malicious web shell sessions or block active privilege escalation attempts locally.

Edge AI is also exceptionally fast. In our own product deployment, edge AI operated in milliseconds rather than taking minutes like agentic LLMs. It can function independently of cloud access or continuous network connectivity. This capabilities shift is crucial for several key reasons:

Even if an attack vector is used to temporarily disable network traffic to operate silently, local edge AI maintains detection coverage

It enables defenders to catch and isolate threats at the earliest possible stage

It safeguards sensitive corporate data locally without requiring telemetry or proprietary data to be transmitted to the cloud for analysis

Despite their speed, agentic AI attackers have a foundational weakness that affects any attacker: They enter your environment without information. To operate, they must perform extensive reconnaissance, port sweeping and environment enumeration. This is where defenders hold the ultimate upper hand through comprehensive AI analytics.

Winning against machine-speed adversaries requires turning the advantage into real-time detection through automated data fusion and detection. By collecting telemetry across diverse network, endpoint, identity and cloud sources, normalization can map these distinct data streams into a single, cohesive schema. Advanced stitching can then merge related events across these disparate sensors into unified, context-rich stories rather than disconnected events.

Observing these integrated stories allows AI analytics engines to establish baselines of normal organizational behavior across the entire ecosystem. While establishing baselines has been longstanding best practice, AI analytics engines have made it easier for defenders to create these and keep them up to date.

Because threat actors lack this context, their automated attempts to rapidly map the terrain inevitably trigger detectors against established baselines, immediately exposing their presence across our detection layers.

Detection alone was never enough to stop machine-speed threats, but the issue is even worse in the era of AI attacks. Detecting an intrusion minutes after it begins still leaves defenders vulnerable if no immediate action is taken, and in those minutes an agentic AI attacker can cause significant damage to your organization. To truly close the loop against these attacks, organizations must combine real-time detection with automated response capabilities, enabling instant attack isolation and automated remediation before critical systems or data can be compromised.

While the sheer volume and speed of AI attacks can seem daunting, there is a positive takeaway: AI-empowered threat actors are not invisible, and their campaigns are entirely detectable. Every attack that we observed triggered alerts, and deep forensic investigations enabled us to reconstruct the attack lifecycles.

The cybersecurity basics still matter. The tools we have relied on as defenders in the past, including XDR solutions, network monitoring and active prevention rules, are still essential and relevant today. We do not need to throw away our playbooks; we simply need to use them differently. By utilizing our existing tools to automate isolation, leaning into AI on the edge for real-time response and trusting our baseline knowledge of our own environments, we are well-equipped to win against the next generation of threats. Crucially, unifying these tools into a cohesive SOC platform connects the dots across the enterprise — where vertical integration and correct platform usage ultimately drive vastly superior security outcomes.

If there’s one thing to take away from this, it is that Mean Time to Detect and Mean Time to Respond (MTTD and MTTR) are still king. But if we want to win these battles, detection and response times must drop to minutes, not hours.

To safeguard your organization against the unprecedented speed and adaptability of AI-driven threats, now is the time to evaluate your defense strategy. Ensure your enterprise is equipped with advanced detection and response capabilities, deploy robust security technologies across your endpoints, network, cloud and identity environments, and continuously refine their configurations to eliminate coverage gaps. Take action today to empower your security operation with the real-time visibility, automated response, and contextual intelligence needed to stay ahead of next-generation adversaries.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score
- [Open source item](https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/ai-threat-horizon/)

### Our 2026 Cybersecurity Predictions: What We Got Right, What We Got Wrong, and What Changed
- Source: Rapid7 Research | Published: 2026-11-25T13:48:40Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ransomware, apt_intrusion, identity, ai_threat
- Summary: Rapid7’s annual Security Predictions webinar returns on December 10, bringing our experts together to explore what the year ahead could hold for cybersecurity. Before sharing our predictions for 2027, we’re grading last year’s calls: that geopolitical tensions would reshape cyber threats, insider threats would dominate breach root causes, and context would become the new currency of cyber performance. Register here for the 2027 Security Predictions webinar . As security leaders work through their priorities for 2027, those predictions offer a useful starting point. The year reinforced several of the concerns we highlighted, while developments in attacker behavior and AI added new dimensions to the discussion. Looking at how those themes unfolded helps us understand what remains relevant and where our thinking needs to evolve. Geopolitics shaped cyber targeting, with businesses caught in 
- Technical details: Rapid7’s annual Security Predictions webinar returns on December 10, bringing our experts together to explore what the year ahead could hold for cybersecurity. Before sharing our predictions for 2027, we’re grading last year’s calls: that geopolitical tensions would reshape cyber threats, insider threats would dominate breach root causes, and context would become the new currency of cyber performance.

Register here for the 2027 Security Predictions webinar .

As security leaders work through their priorities for 2027, those predictions offer a useful starting point. The year reinforced several of the concerns we highlighted, while developments in attacker behavior and AI added new dimensions to the discussion. Looking at how those themes unfolded helps us understand what remains relevant and where our thinking needs to evolve.

Our prediction that geopolitical fault lines would redraw the cyber battlefield earned an A-, as regional conflicts created opportunities for espionage, disruption, and public pressure throughout 2026. Cyber operations allowed adversaries to pursue those objectives below the threshold of open warfare, with commercial organizations exposed alongside government and critical infrastructure targets.

Rapid7 Intelligence tracked a spike in retaliatory activity following regional military escalations in late February. State-directed actors pursued espionage and data theft, while hacktivist personas amplified the impact. Rapid7 also assessed that stolen information was likely to be passed from state actors to hacktivist groups for public release, extending the consequences of an intrusion beyond the initial compromise.

That chain from state actor to hacktivist release makes an organization’s exposure harder to judge from its own location or industry alone. Customers, suppliers, infrastructure, and perceived political associations can attract attention, bringing a seemingly routine commercial relationship into a much wider conflict. Examining those dependencies during response exercises uncovers difficult decisions early, particularly where protecting a service or containing an incident requires agreement across security, operations, legal, and executive leadership.

Our prediction that insider threats would dominate breach root causes drew attention to risks ranging from employee negligence to deliberate access selling. During 2026, reporting on ransomware recruitment attempts reinforced concerns about attackers seeking cooperation from people inside organizations, while the growing use of AI agents broadened the conversation about insider risk. As agents gain access to business systems and take actions on an organization’s behalf, questions about permissions, oversight, and unintended consequences increasingly extend beyond human users.

Our blog on insider recruitment in ransomware campaigns explored an approach by Medusa to a BBC journalist, offering payment in exchange for access. It illustrated how attackers combine financial incentives, persuasion, and pressure to pursue legitimate credentials, giving organizations a concrete reason to include recruitment attempts in awareness programs and response planning.

The wider access economy also remained significant, although credentials appearing for sale do not establish that an employee willingly supplied them. External compromise and exposed systems continued to provide routes into businesses, with AI-assisted vulnerability discovery adding further pressure. Our prediction captured an important concern, while the year reinforced the need to distinguish deliberate insider cooperation from other ways attackers obtain and misuse access.

Our prediction that context would become the new currency of cyber performance earned a B, largely because understanding an environment has always been fundamental to protecting it. During a year shaped by AI-driven vulnerability discovery, however, the ability to connect a new finding with exposed systems, attacker activity, and business consequences became particularly valuable when teams had little time to decide how to respond.

Our analysis of the ASOS incident explored that challenge after an unauthorized notification reached customers through the retailer’s own app. With the route of compromise still unclear, the incident raised questions about access to customer communications platforms and how investigators connect activity across accounts, integrations, and third-party services.

The same need for context applies to remediation, where an advisory alone cannot determine whether interrupting a critical service is justified. Teams need to know where affected technology is running, how attackers could reach it, and which mitigations are practical. Bringing asset information, threat intelligence, and operational knowledge together remains essential to making those decisions with confidence.

Across all three themes, the year reinforced how closely security decisions depend on understanding relationships: between organizations, between identities and access, and between technical weaknesses and business consequences. Those connections will remain relevant as AI changes how technology is used and how threats develop.

Join Rapid7’s Security Predictions webinar on December 10 to explore those shifts with our experts and consider what they could mean for your priorities in the year ahead.

Rapid7s curated database of vulnerabilities, featuring exploit modules and check methods integrated into the Metasploit Framework.

The threat research behind the alerts: adversary tracking, curated intelligence, and flagship threat reports.

Gain 24x7 XDR monitoring, remediation, and DFIR from experts that extend your team to help secure your extended ecosystem.

For details on how to apply this update, which includes the changes described in this advisory, refer to:

The system must be rebooted for this update to take effect.

Red Hat recommends treating all kernel errata as security-relevant. Given the kernel's fundamental role, any bug has a higher chance of impacting system security, even if that impact only becomes clear after a fix is published. Therefore, Red Hat prioritizes delivering fixes that improve our customers' overall security posture.

Because of this proactive approach, a patch may be associated with a CVE assignment at a future date. Retroactive CVE assignments are always documented in the corresponding errata and on Red Hat's CVE pages. We strongly advise against delaying updates, as doing so may leave your system exposed when protections are already available.

### Software giant SAP confirms cyber incident amid reports of source code breach
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-27T13:15:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: German software giant SAP confirmed a cybersecurity incident following reports that hackers infiltrated its software development infrastructure, potentially gaining access to the company’s source code.
- Business background / implications: German software giant SAP confirmed a cybersecurity incident following reports that hackers infiltrated its software development infrastructure, potentially gaining access to the company’s source code.

In a statement to Recorded Future News on Friday, SAP said it had identified suspicious activity and immediately taken steps to protect its customers and secure its services. The company did not disclose the nature or extent of the intrusion.

“Based on our investigation to date, we have found no evidence of unauthorized access to customer business data,” SAP said. “We will continue to strengthen our security to provide the secure and resilient environment our customers depend on.”

German magazine Der Spiegel first reported the incident Thursday, citing internal company communications and sources familiar with the matter.

According to the magazine, hackers targeted SAP’s software development environment hosted on GitHub, obtaining employee authentication tokens that allowed them to copy and modify the company’s source code.

The German Press Agency (dpa), which independently reviewed portions of internal SAP communication, reported that the company had alerted employees to suspicious activity within its GitHub environment several days before the incident became public. Employees were advised to assess whether they needed to change their login credentials or take additional security precautions.

SAP launched an internal incident response operation code-named “Project Granite,” according to German business newspaper Handelsblatt.

The company’s emergency security measures reportedly affected several software development teams. SAP restricted access to GitHub, halted automated development processes and replaced digital keys used to verify that its software was genuine and had not been tampered with.

It remains unclear whether the hackers accessed SAP’s proprietary source code or whether any altered code ended up in software delivered to customers.

SAP has not publicly identified who was behind the attack, how the hackers gained access to its systems or whether the incident has been fully contained.

SAP is one of the world’s largest enterprise software companies whose products are used by businesses and public institutions worldwide to manage critical operations, including accounting, procurement, supply chains and human resources.

The incident follows a separate software supply chain attack in April, when hackers injected malicious code into several ope
